    cs.CV cs.AI

    Luggage re-identification: A study of strategies towards a real-world application

    Authors: Quentin Dhôte, Romaric Audigier

    Abstract: …background and crowded scenes which significantly degrade performance. Yet, strategies leveraging the complementarity of multiple shots and views from different video streams is overlooked. In this paper, we study strategies for an end-to-end pipeline that operates from multiple video streams to detect and re-identify… ▽ More Luggage re-identification (ReID) is a critical task in video surveillance for forensics, security or border management (suspicious or abandoned luggage recognition), as well as luggage management (lost luggage recovery). It enables the association of observations of the same luggage across disjoint camera views in complex environments such as airports, train stations and border crossing. While existing approaches primarily focus on appearance-based cropped image matching under relatively clean conditions, real-world scenarios are characterized by frequent occlusions, cluttered background and crowded scenes which significantly degrade performance. Yet, strategies leveraging the complementarity of multiple shots and views from different video streams is overlooked. In this paper, we study strategies for an end-to-end pipeline that operates from multiple video streams to detect and re-identify luggage. We evaluate different variants on a benchmark dataset acquired in real-world environments to address the questions of selecting and merging different views and shots of the same luggage as well as training on different environment datasets for an overall ReID performance improvement in practical applications. △ Less

    Submitted 29 October, 2026; originally announced October 2026.

    Comments: 7 pages, 4 figures, 5 tables. Accepted at The 2026 IEEE International Conference on Content-Based Multimedia Indexing (IEEE CBMI)

  2. arXiv:2610.40636  [pdf, ps, other] 

    cs.CV cs.AI

    Probing Spatial State Representations in Visual Foundation Models

    Authors: Baiqiao Yin, Zhongang Cai, Ruisi Wang, Qiwei Liang, Lei Yang, Ziwei Liu

    Abstract: …levels of spatial capability---spatial structure, object persistence, predictive spatial update, and actionability---using frozen-feature probes on ten representations under streaming egocentric observations of static indoor environments. The resulting capability profiles vary across tasks, network layers, and prefix lengths: strong readout of the visible sc… ▽ More An observer sees only part of the surrounding world, yet must keep track of what lies beyond the current view. Spatial perception therefore involves more than recognizing individual images: it calls for a persistent spatial state that is updated under self-motion and used to guide action. Do visual foundation models provide representations that support such a state? We study this question through four levels of spatial capability---spatial structure, object persistence, predictive spatial update, and actionability---using frozen-feature probes on ten representations under streaming egocentric observations of static indoor environments. The resulting capability profiles vary across tasks, network layers, and prefix lengths: strong readout of the visible scene does not ensure accurate object persistence or future-feature retrieval. Matched controls separate readout accuracy from reliance on visual history and motion. Native future prediction retains recoverable spatial structure, but a spatial reader trained on real features does not transfer to predicted features unchanged. Finally, goal-dependent candidate selection does not establish reliable continuous action utility: predicted actions respond to goal changes, yet supplying the correct goal does not reliably improve progress. Across the four levels, object persistence and actionability build on camera-pose readout, whereas feature prediction builds on visible-scene readout. Together, these findings motivate evaluating spatial representations not only by what can be decoded from them, but by whether that information is maintained beyond observation, updated predictively, and used for goal-directed action. △ Less

    Submitted 29 October, 2026; originally announced October 2026.

    cs.RO cs.CV

    SymAnt: Grounding Long-Horizon Action Anticipation with Adaptable Symbolic Priors

    Authors: Fnu Aryan, Yaqi Xie, Joseph Campbell, Woojun Kim, Kwonjoon Lee, Hossein Nourkhiz Mahjoub, Ehsan Moradi-Pari, Katia Sycara, Simon Stepputtis

    Abstract: As robots and intelligent systems increasingly interact with humans, they must anticipate long… ▽ More As robots and intelligent systems increasingly interact with humans, they must anticipate long sequences of future actions from only brief observations. While purely neural models typically require extensive observations to predict future actions and remain difficult to interpret and adjust, symbolic priors offer a structured alternative. To this end, we introduce SymAnt, a novel neuro-symbolic approach that unifies the expressiveness of neural networks with the structured reasoning of scene and knowledge graphs. Given a short video, SymAnt performs a joint-search over both graphs to construct a symbolic context, enabling unified reasoning over spatial relationships, object attributes, affordances, and associated actions. This symbolic context serves as a prior for an iterative refinement process that progressively generates and improves the sequence of future actions. By grounding predictions in symbolic context, SymAnt anticipates long-horizon actions from short observation windows in a parameter-efficient manner, while enabling users to directly edit the underlying knowledge to adjust predictions without further training. Through our experiments, we demonstrate the effectiveness of SymAnt, particularly when only short observation contexts are available, across five diverse datasets: Breakfast, 50 Salads, EPIC-KITCHENS, EGTEA Gaze+, and Assembly101. △ Less

    Submitted 29 October, 2026; originally announced October 2026.

    Comments: Accepted to WACV 2027. 18 pages (8-page main paper, references, and supplementary appendix), 6 figures, 9 tables. Project page: https://aryanmangal769.github.io/symant/

    cs.SE cs.CL cs.LG

    One to More, More to One: Category-Aware Iterative Expert Training for Software Engineering Agents

    Authors: Jie Zhao, Ziyu Jiang, Suhang Zheng, Minghui Shan, Xiaoxiao Xu, Lin Qu

    Abstract: …while leaving uneven instance-level progress, motivating explicit consolidation of successful behavior and policy-adaptive task selection. Same-origin category experts alternate long-horizon Agentic-miniRL with Refresh-Repair-Expand (RRE): the updated policy refreshes instance mastery, reuses its own verified successfu… ▽ More Repository-level software engineering (SWE) comprises heterogeneous task categories, whose progress under pooled agentic reinforcement learning can be uneven: gains in some categories coincide with regressions in others, while aggregate resolution obscures these changes. Motivated by this category see-saw, we develop a category-aware expert-training and policy-integration framework. Executable task construction and SWE Labeler, an evidence-grounded multi-axis labeling system, organize the training pools. Initial category-specific RL improves average training success while leaving uneven instance-level progress, motivating explicit consolidation of successful behavior and policy-adaptive task selection. Same-origin category experts alternate long-horizon Agentic-miniRL with Refresh-Repair-Expand (RRE): the updated policy refreshes instance mastery, reuses its own verified successful trajectories for Repair SFT, and reselects tasks for further RL. Label-routed multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) consolidates the experts into one deployable student, with ReLU-gated reward extrapolation keeping only each teacher's improving direction over the reference. Expert training and policy integration require no external model to provide solution trajectories or action targets. On a fixed, audit-filtered Pro-618 subset, we connect Pooled RL and Balanced RL, expert development, and single-model integration through overall and per-category resolution and expert-gain recovery. In final-model evaluations, MOPD achieves mean resolution of 86.19% on SWE-bench Pro V2 and 71.78% on SWE-bench Multilingual, exceeding the base model by 4.57 and 3.89 percentage points, respectively. The same three 27B experts also improve a Qwen3.6-35B-A3B student across the evaluated task sets. △ Less

    Submitted 29 October, 2026; v1 submitted 20 September, 2026; originally announced September 2026.

    Comments: 47 pages, 12 figures. v2: added evaluations on SWE-bench Pro (731 tasks), SWE-bench Pro V2/Hard-51, and LiveCodeBench v6 (175 tasks); updated SWE-bench Multilingual results; added 35B-A3B student experiments. Code: https://github.com/alibaba/AgenticBigBang

  5. arXiv:2610.40614  [pdf, ps, other] 

    cs.CV

    L3G-World: Real-Time Infinite World Generation with Latent 3D Gaussian Memory

    Authors: Jinhyeok Choi, JoungBin Lee, Jongmin Lee, Minkyeong Jeon, Jiyoung Kim, Kaeun Han, Yoonjin Yoon, Seungryong Kim

    Abstract: Interactive world models generate visual content conditioned on user actions, yet maintaining spatial consistency over long-… ▽ More Interactive world models generate visual content conditioned on user actions, yet maintaining spatial consistency over long-horizon exploration in real time remains challenging. Existing approaches rely on either temporal context or explicit 3D memory, both of which scale poorly because memory grows with the length of the generated stream. Moreover, explicit 3D memories condition latent video generators by re-encoding RGB renderings, which precludes real-time generation. In this paper, we introduce a Latent 3D Gaussian Memory (L3G) that combines the efficiency of feed-forward reconstruction with a compact, rapidly renderable scene representation. Building on this memory, we propose L3G-World, in which a memory updater and a video diffusion transformer (DiT) alternate autoregressively to generate spatially consistent worlds. The memory updater spawns Gaussians in newly observed regions and refines existing ones, so that the memory grows with scene extent rather than stream length. Each Gaussian further stores a VAE latent feature, allowing the memory to be rendered directly into the DiT's latent space without VAE re-encoding. Experiments show that L3G-World achieves state-of-the-art generation quality and long-horizon spatial consistency, with up to $38.6\times$ fewer 3D primitives, $3.2\times$ faster memory updates, and $359\times$ faster memory rendering relative to the most efficient prior 3D-memory baseline for each metric, enabling real-time world generation at 9 FPS. △ Less

    Submitted 29 October, 2026; originally announced October 2026.

    Comments: Project page: https://cvlab-kaist.github.io/L3G-World/

    cs.CV cs.GR

    HairDeck: Animation-Ready Stylized Hair Cards from a Single Image

    Authors: Yuze He, Yixiang Wei, Wang Zhao, Chuhao Chen, Yanning Zhou, Yong-Jin Liu

    Abstract: …Existing image-based methods produce realistic hair or a single fused mesh, while CHARM, the only generator of stylized hair cards, decodes a hairstyle autoregressively as a long sequence of control points. Observing that a card hairstyle is an unordered set of artist-defined primitives, we propose HairDeck, which gen… ▽ More The hair of stylized characters in games and avatars is built from hair cards: artist-placed ribbons whose geometry is the visible hair, animated by spring bones. Existing image-based methods produce realistic hair or a single fused mesh, while CHARM, the only generator of stylized hair cards, decodes a hairstyle autoregressively as a long sequence of control points. Observing that a card hairstyle is an unordered set of artist-defined primitives, we propose HairDeck, which generates it as one: each card is a single token, and a set diffusion transformer denoises all cards together. From a single image, HairDeck generates the cards and the spring-bone chains that drive them, yielding an animatable asset that exports to ready-to-use downstream formats. On CHARM's own test set, it lowers Chamfer distance by 44% and card-orientation error by more than half while running 33 times faster, and from single images it outperforms CHARM, image-to-3D pipelines and strand-based methods. Project page: https://hyzcluster.github.io/HairDeck △ Less

    cs.RO

    DAWN: Diffusion-based Alignment and unfastening Workflow Network

    Authors: Hemant Kumar, Piyush Goenka, Nikhil Chopra

    Abstract: …challenges in precise screwdriver engagement with fasteners, detailed force execution, and robustness to environmental variability. To address the complexity of learning long-horizon, contact-rich manipulation tasks, we subdivide the unfastening task procedure into two sequential stages: (1) alignment & insertion,… ▽ More We present DAWN (Diffusion-based Alignment and unfastening Workflow Network), a hierarchical robotic framework designed to perform real-world, unfastening tasks through multi-modal sensory integration. Autonomous robotic unfastening is crucial for improving recycling processes, enhancing safety, and reducing operational costs; yet, it remains predominantly manual due to inherent challenges in precise screwdriver engagement with fasteners, detailed force execution, and robustness to environmental variability. To address the complexity of learning long-horizon, contact-rich manipulation tasks, we subdivide the unfastening task procedure into two sequential stages: (1) alignment & insertion, and (2) unfastening. DAWN consists of three key components: AlignNet, a diffusion-based policy ensuring precise screwdriver tip engagement; LatchNet, a multi-modal classifier reliably detecting successful stage transitions; and UnfastNet, which achieves stable, force-controlled screw removal. Experimental validation on a Franka Emika robot demonstrates that DAWN achieves alignment & insertion success rates comparable to trained human operators, robustly transitions between stages, and attains an impressive success rate in unfastening across various unseen scenarios. Ablation studies further elucidate critical input modalities and effective training strategies, highlighting DAWN's scalability, robustness, and data efficiency. △ Less

    Comments: 8 pages, including references

    math.NA cs.LG physics.comp-ph physics.plasm-ph

    NEOPIC: A Neural Operator Framework for Particle-based Kinetic Plasma Simulations

    Authors: Sriramkrishnan Muralikrishnan, Chelsea Maria John, Jeffrey Kelling, Michael Bussmann, Robert Speck, Paul Gibbon

    Abstract: …set by the architecture alone, independently of any mesh or tree. Numerical experiments on electrostatic benchmarks in two and three dimensions, including Landau damping, two-stream and bump-on-tail instabilities, a cyclotron mini-app and a Penning trap, confirm the theoretical findings. A single model trained on several mini-apps generated by different refe… ▽ More We introduce NEOPIC, a framework for learning the field operator in particle-based methods applied to kinetic plasma simulations. Its key novelty is a neural operator that acts directly on the empirical measure of the particles, rather than on a grid, and is evaluated at the particle positions. No mesh or tree is needed and, unlike grid-based neural operators, no deposition onto a mesh takes place, so the particle noise that dominates the inputs of particle simulations is not aliased. Since it replaces only the field solve, it serves as a plug-and-play field solver for particle-based methods: it can be trained on data from particle-in-cell, particle-in-Fourier or tree codes, and combined with any time integrator, including implicit schemes. We prove that the discrete evaluation coincides with the continuous operator at any number of particles (representation equivalence) and derive an error analysis that separates sampling, learning and time-stepping errors: up to the learning error, NEOPIC has the error structure of standard Monte Carlo particle methods. We show that the particle noise in the training data acts as an implicit regularizer, which gives guidance on the number of training particles in relation to the particle numbers used at inference. A complexity analysis shows that NEOPIC is an $O(N_p)$ method whose constants are set by the architecture alone, independently of any mesh or tree. Numerical experiments on electrostatic benchmarks in two and three dimensions, including Landau damping, two-stream and bump-on-tail instabilities, a cyclotron mini-app and a Penning trap, confirm the theoretical findings. A single model trained on several mini-apps generated by different reference methods captures all of them and remains stable up to twice the training time window, which points towards foundation models for fields in particle-based methods. △ Less

    cs.AI cs.LG

    VoiceLongMemEval: Do Assistants Remember How You Sounded?

    Authors: Ramit Pahwa, Parivesh Priye, Apoorva Beedu

    Abstract: …increasingly tasked with reasoning over long, continuous, multi-session conversation histories. Current benchmarks evaluate this dialogue history as information retrieval over long horizon, temporal reasoning, or knowledge updates, while crucially ignoring the fundamental dynamics of human-agent interaction, i.e. how t… ▽ More With the growing scale of multi-agent architectures and large language models, deployed AI assistants are increasingly tasked with reasoning over long, continuous, multi-session conversation histories. Current benchmarks evaluate this dialogue history as information retrieval over long horizon, temporal reasoning, or knowledge updates, while crucially ignoring the fundamental dynamics of human-agent interaction, i.e. how they said it. To address this gap, we present VoiceLongMemEval (VLME) benchmark, where every answer depends on paralinguistic metadata (emotion labels, prosody descriptors, and voice events) attached to conversational turns, which is otherwise unrecoverable from the words alone. Every item passes a three-stage adversarial gate, ensuring that a strong language model fails when given only the transcript. Evaluating leading frontier and open-weight models reveals a pervasive affect gap; providing text-track paralinguistic metadata yields a 0.09 to 0.38 accuracy boost (0.61 to 0.69 when prompted with evidence hints), while standard ASR pipelines systematically discard this signal. Additionally, audio-native models successfully extract these cues directly from speech (0.354 to 0.412 vs. 0.325 blind). Code and dataset will be made available upon acceptance. △ Less

    cs.CV

    GameVerse: A Minute-Scale Gameplay Dataset for Long-Horizon Interactive World Modeling

    Authors: Kang He, Wenshuo Peng, Chuanhao Li, Zihui Gao, Xiaojie Xu, Zhengyuan Lin, Yuzhe Ding, Donghong Ji, Kaipeng Zhang, Yongtao Ge

    Abstract: …from action inputs. Yet current models struggle to maintain coherence beyond roughly one minute of rollout, a limitation rooted not in model capacity but in the scarcity of long-… ▽ More Recent advances in interactive world models have made it possible to generate controllable, dynamic environments from action inputs. Yet current models struggle to maintain coherence beyond roughly one minute of rollout, a limitation rooted not in model capacity but in the scarcity of long-horizon training data. Existing gameplay video datasets typically consist of clips under ten seconds, with captions that describe whole clips in coarse and undifferentiated text, which is insufficient for controllable long-horizon generation. We address this with GameVerse, a minute-scale gameplay video dataset purpose-built for long-horizon interactive world modeling. GameVerse contains 69.7K clips spanning 1,655 hours and 349.6 million frames across diverse first- and third-person gameplay, including 1,358 hours of continuous two-minute sequences, an order of magnitude longer than prior datasets. Clips pass through a unified pipeline of scene-consistent segmentation, quality filtering, camera pose estimation, and event-based caption annotation. Each clip is paired with a temporally stable camera trajectory that remains coherent across minutes, together with captions that separate static scene context from dynamic foreground events and align to event boundaries rather than fixed time windows. Comprehensive analysis shows that GameVerse surpasses existing public datasets in scale, diversity, and annotation quality. Experiments further demonstrate that training on GameVerse substantially extends the temporal horizon of interactive world models. △ Less

    Submitted 29 October, 2026; v1 submitted 27 October, 2026; originally announced October 2026.

    Comments: Accepted by NeurIPS 2026

    cs.LG cs.AI

    Towards Batch-to-Streaming Deep Reinforcement Learning for Continuous Control

    Authors: Riccardo De Monte, Matteo Cederle, Gian Antonio Susto

    Abstract: …is often incompatible with the constraints of resource-limited hardware, due to their reliance on replay buffers, batch updates, and target networks. The emerging paradigm of streaming deep RL addresses this limitation through purely online updates, achieving strong empirical performance on standard benchmarks. In this work, we propose two novel… ▽ More State-of-the-art deep reinforcement learning (RL) methods have achieved remarkable performance in continuous control tasks, yet their computational complexity is often incompatible with the constraints of resource-limited hardware, due to their reliance on replay buffers, batch updates, and target networks. The emerging paradigm of streaming deep RL addresses this limitation through purely online updates, achieving strong empirical performance on standard benchmarks. In this work, we propose two novel streaming deep RL algorithms, Streaming Soft Actor-Critic (S2AC) and Streaming Deterministic Actor-Critic (SDAC), explicitly designed to be compatible with state-of-the-art batch RL methods, making them particularly suitable for on-device finetuning applications such as Sim2Real transfer. Both algorithms achieve performance comparable to state-of-the-art streaming baselines on standard benchmarks without requiring tedious per-environment hyperparameter tuning. We further investigate the batch-to-streaming transition, showing that a naive transition does not guarantee preservation of pre-trained policy performance, and propose a principled approach to address this challenge. △ Less

    physics.comp-ph

    Predicting Weight Windows for Duct-Streaming Shielding Geometries with a Convolutional Neural Network

    Authors: Jilang Miao, Ansel Kneier, Jonathan Porrazzo

    Abstract: Forward-flux-based weight windows give global variance reduction for duct-streaming shielding problems, but each new geometry needs an iterative Monte Carlo calculation to build them. We report preliminary results on a convolutional neural network (CNN) that learns the map from a two-dimensional duct geometry and source position to the lower window bound of… ▽ More Forward-flux-based weight windows give global variance reduction for duct-streaming shielding problems, but each new geometry needs an iterative Monte Carlo calculation to build them. We report preliminary results on a convolutional neural network (CNN) that learns the map from a two-dimensional duct geometry and source position to the lower window bound of such an iteration, here the MAGIC generator in OpenMC, in milliseconds. A set of 250 random concrete ducts of three curved and rectangular families on the Protvino labyrinth bounding box was run in OpenMC with a Cf-252 point source and MAGIC generation, and a 1.9-million-parameter U-Net was trained on the logarithm of the lower bound. On held-out ducts the error in the cells that carry the flux is 0.14 to 0.51 decades; replicate MAGIC runs show this part of the map to be reproducible to a few hundredths of a decade, so the residual is model error, while below $10^{-6}$ the reference itself is noise. Applied without retraining to the rectangular Protvino labyrinth, the prediction correlates with MAGIC at 0.96. Used as weight windows there, with its deepest cells clamped at deployment, it gives an exit-detector figure of merit 54 times the analog value, 1.7 times that of the MAGIC generation run for the same geometry, and 0.80 of that of reused MAGIC windows whose construction it does not pay for. The run cost of any window set is shown to be set by its deepest cells and predictable from a population proxy. △ Less

    Submitted 29 October, 2026; originally announced October 2026.

    Comments: 11 pages, 3 figures, 5 tables. Submitted to M&C 2027, the International Conference on Mathematics and Computational Methods Applied to Nuclear Science and Engineering (American Nuclear Society)

Existing methods often rely on masked inputs, generation-based pairs, or training pairs with limited viewpoint variation, leaving pose and illumination adaptation and bidirectional object-scene interactions insufficiently constrained.     From Action Principle to Stochastic Thermodynamics of Elastic and Viscous Active Matter

    Authors: Valentina Barletta, Michele Lupini, Ian D. Estabrook, Laeschkir Würthner, Isabella R. Graf, Caren Norden, Lamberto Rondoni, Anna Erzberger, Roman Belousov

    Abstract: …from the classical principle of least action and the Rayleighian formalism for external forces. Our approach couples the local barycentric collective coordinate and the streaming velocity as the canonical conjugate pair, which unifies various descriptions of elastic and viscous soft matter, including Helfrich theory for lipid membranes, linear and nonlinear… ▽ More Motivated by the complex material properties of living matter, we derive a framework of stochastic thermo- and elastodynamics for active materials from the classical principle of least action and the Rayleighian formalism for external forces. Our approach couples the local barycentric collective coordinate and the streaming velocity as the canonical conjugate pair, which unifies various descriptions of elastic and viscous soft matter, including Helfrich theory for lipid membranes, linear and nonlinear elasticity of thin shells and deformable droplets, as well as hydrodynamics of active and passive fluids. The Lagrangian mechanics of collective field variables permits direct applications of the results and theorems from statistical mechanics to existing models of complex soft and living matter. To illustrate the potential applications of stochastic elastodynamics in biology, we derive and simulate a boundary-driven deformation model of an elastic cell nucleus in the presence of fluctuations. △ Less

  15. arXiv:2610.40350  [pdf, ps, other] 

    cs.CL

    Don't Lose the Thread: Empowering Long-Horizon LLM Agents with Cognitive Resource Self-Allocation

    Authors: Zheng Zhu, Xiaozhuan Liang, Xi Chen, Bei Yu, Jiaya Jia

    Abstract: Agents powered by large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable progress in solving complex reasoning tasks. However, LLM agents often falter on long-… ▽ More Agents powered by large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable progress in solving complex reasoning tasks. However, LLM agents often falter on long-horizon tasks due to cognitive overload, as their working memory becomes cluttered with expanding and irrelevant information, which dilutes their attention and hinders effective planning and reasoning. To mitigate this challenge, we introduce Cognitive Resource Self-Allocation (CORAL), a novel reasoning paradigm that empowers agents to proactively optimize their context. Implemented as an agent-callable working memory management toolset, CORAL allows an agent to checkpoint its task progress and verified facts, and adaptively initiate a new problem-solving episode by resetting cluttered context while preserving only the checkpointed information, enabling it to resume reasoning from a clean state. We further enhance the agent's checkpointing capabilities using a Multi-episode Agentic Reinforced Policy Optimization algorithm. On the GAIA benchmark, CORAL with an 8B model achieves 42.8% average accuracy, with particularly strong gains on the most challenging long-horizon tasks (Level 2 and Level 3). Crucially, these gains come at a substantially lower inference cost: CORAL consumes 6-7$\times$ fewer input tokens than a ReAct agent. It also generalizes to multi-hop Knowledge QA benchmarks and to the considerably harder BrowseComp, where it improves a strong proprietary agent by 8.0 points. △ Less

    Submitted 29 October, 2026; originally announced October 2026.

    Comments: Accepted at COLM 2026. Code is available at https://github.com/JIA-Lab-research/CORAL

  16. arXiv:2605.12655  [pdf, ps, other] 

    cs.AI cs.MA

    Robust Instruction Compliance in Cooperative Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning

    Authors: Wo Wei Lin, Ethan Rathbun, Enrico Marchesini, Xiang Zhi Tan

    Abstract: Multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) in real-world use cases may need to adapt to external natural language instructions that interrupt ongoing behavior and conflict with long-horizon objectives. However, conditioning rewards on instructions introduces a fundamental failure mode as Bellman updates couple value est… ▽ More Multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) in real-world use cases may need to adapt to external natural language instructions that interrupt ongoing behavior and conflict with long-horizon objectives. However, conditioning rewards on instructions introduces a fundamental failure mode as Bellman updates couple value estimates across instruction contexts, leading to inconsistent values when instructions interrupt macro-actions. We propose Macro-Action Value Correction for Instruction Compliance (MAVIC), which corrects Bellman backups at instruction boundaries by correcting the incoming instruction objective and restoring the continuation value under the current objective. Unlike reward shaping, MAVIC modifies the bootstrapping target itself, enabling consistent value estimation under stochastic instruction switching within a unified policy. We provide theoretical analysis and an actor-critic implementation, and show that MAVIC achieves high instruction compliance while preserving base task performance in increasingly complex cooperative multi-agent environments. △ Less

  17. arXiv:2608.20084  [pdf, ps, other] 

    cs.RO cs.AI

    Evidence-Gated Task and Motion Planning with Vision-Language Models

    Authors: Tsunehiko Tanaka, Matthew Stephenson, Alistair Macvicar, Edgar Simo-Serra

    Abstract: Robots executing long-horizon manipulation tasks from natural-language instructions must reason about both semantic task structure and geometric feasibility. Hierarchical approaches combining Vision-Language Models (VLMs) with Task and Motion Planning (TAMP) offer a promising solution, but VLM-generated subgoals may re… ▽ More Robots executing long-horizon manipulation tasks from natural-language instructions must reason about both semantic task structure and geometric feasibility. Hierarchical approaches combining Vision-Language Models (VLMs) with Task and Motion Planning (TAMP) offer a promising solution, but VLM-generated subgoals may rely on prior knowledge without sufficient observational support. This can lead to the omission of task-relevant subgoals or attempts to execute instructions whose requirements cannot be met in the environment. We propose Evidence Acquisition and Feasibility Gating (EAFG), a framework for evidence-gated task and motion planning that acquires visual evidence to specify appropriate task plans and assess whether given instructions can be fulfilled. EAFG first acquires evidence by having a VLM generate subgoals and a robot execute action sequences planned by TAMP for these subgoals. For feasibility gating, the acquired evidence is used to decide whether to proceed with task planning, acquire further evidence, or halt. When proceeding, the VLM generates task subgoals conditioned on the acquired evidence, and TAMP computes low-level actions. We evaluate EAFG in three cooking scenarios involving explicitly specified required objects, underspecified instructions, and instructions requiring an absent object. EAFG improves task completion over a VLM-TAMP baseline in the explicit and underspecified scenarios and enables appropriate halting when a required object is absent. △ Less

    Learned Terminal Costs for Short-Horizon Model Predictive Control of Autonomous Trucks in Interactive Highway Traffic

    Authors: Hooman Hamze, Benjamin Houshmand, Deepthi Pathare, Erik Börve, Morteza Haghir Chehreghani, Nikolce Murgovski

Model Predictive Control (MPC) provides rigorous constraint satisfaction but relies on terminal cost which can l… ▽ More Autonomous highway driving for heavy-duty trucks is a challenging control problem. The greater mass, dimensions and intricate dynamics increase the requirements for cooperation from surrounding traffic in critical maneuvers, requiring a careful balance between safety constraints and efficient long-horizon decision making. Model Predictive Control (MPC) provides rigorous constraint satisfaction but relies on terminal cost which can limit its effectiveness at short, computationally affordable horizons. Reinforcement Learning (RL) captures long-term driving behavior but offers no constraint guarantees and tends toward overly conservative policies when trained under sparse collision penalties. In this paper, we propose a hybrid MPC and RL framework for highway driving in autonomous trucks, where a finite-horizon MPC, solved via dynamic programming, uses a pre-trained RL model for terminal cost estimation. We evaluate the framework in a simulation environment with reactive surrounding traffic and compare it against pure MPC with zero terminal cost, rollout with terminal cost estimation using a base policy, and pure RL. Results show that the hybrid approach achieves zero collisions with the lowest cost of any configuration. △ Less

However, current works lack specification in both quantity and diversity, often emphasizing primary goals while ignoring complex constraints. Synthesizing tasks to bridge this gap presents two challenges: naively adding specifications risks reducing tasks to trivial checklists rather than compelling joint reasoning, and verifying these subtle interactions risks overlooking hidden flaws or rejecting valid alternatives. To address these challenges, we introduce CodeSpecWeave, a framework for synthesizing tasks with rich, interdependent specifications. Grounded in real developer seeds, a synthesis agent iteratively develops both coupled task requirements and executable tests, while a review agent audits solution trajectories from multiple models to guarantee test reliability. Using this pipeline, we construct 4,670 training tasks across 2,865 repositories. Applying Reinforcement learning on these tasks significantly improves performance over the base model on DeepSWE-v1.1, largely outperforming existing baselines. Furthermore, we curate CodeSpecWeave-Bench, a rigorous benchmark comprising 100 tasks and 1,044 specifications, which clearly separates frontier models and reveals significant headroom in handling interdependent engineering constraints. △ Less

We further enhance the framework by introducing class-conditional guarantees to ensure… ▽ More Artificial intelligence (AI) models can accelerate cell-type annotation in digital pathology, but safely scaling their use requires determining, with error control, which model-generated labels can be accepted without expert review. This is challenging in evolving workflows because cell morphology and type composition may change across tissue regions and slides, making one-time calibration unreliable. We propose Conformal Annotation with Randomized Expert review under Distribution shift (CARED), a distribution-free framework that leverages randomized expert audit to achieve long-run error control for AI-assisted annotation under distribution shift. For each incoming batch, CARED uniformly selects a subset of cases for expert review and uses the acquired labels to calibrate later decisions. Among unreviewed cases, CARED determines a subset of them to safely skip expert labeling, while suggesting candidate labels for the others for downstream resolution. We prove that this workflow provides long-run control of cumulative error over both the full stream and the cells outside of the audit sample, without stationarity or exchangeability assumptions. We further enhance the framework by introducing class-conditional guarantees to ensure error control within important but rare cell types, together with a utility module to target more automatic resolutions, smaller candidate sets, and greater biological coherence, without compromising validity. Experiments on pancreas, lung, and ovary annotation streams, together with a stream constructed from real expert annotations, show that CARED controls the cumulative errors while a substantial fraction of cells (up to 70%) bypass expert review. △ Less

    Authors: Long Zhou, Luca Zanella, Paolo Rota, Massimiliano Mancini, Elisa Ricci

    Abstract: …understanding capabilities of Large Multimodal Models. To construct elementary temporal units, HSD incrementally groups incomi… ▽ More Given a set of procedural steps, Video Step Grounding (VSG) aims to identify which steps occur in a video and localize when they are performed. Existing methods are either offline, requiring access to the full video before making predictions, or online, but relying on fixed-length video segments producing noisy, over-segmented outputs. In this work, we propose Hierarchical Step Decomposition (HSD), a training-free framework for online VSG that leverages the semantic understanding capabilities of Large Multimodal Models. HSD addresses two central challenges in online grounding: constructing semantically coherent temporal segments from a streaming video, and bridging the semantic gap between coarse procedural step descriptions and visual observations. To construct elementary temporal units, HSD incrementally groups incoming segments into semantically consistent events, using an anchor caption that is continuously validated against the visual stream. Based on these events, we perform zero-shot procedural step classification and selectively refine uncertain predictions by decomposing candidate steps into finegrained substeps that capture more nuanced visual evidence. By operating on semantically coherent events rather than every fixed-length segment, HSD reduces redundant computation and produces more stable predictions. Experiments on HT-Step, Ego4D Goal-Step, and CrossTask show that HSD substantially outperforms state-of-the-art methods, overall achieving better step recognition, more accurate temporal localization, and faster inference. △ Less

    cs.LG cs.AI

    Escaping Path Mirages in Offline Goal-Conditioned Reinforcement Learning

    Authors: Junhyeon Bae, Jaebak Hwang, Gwanwoo Choi, Minung Kim, Seungyul Han

    Abstract: Offline goal-conditioned reinforcement learning remains challenging in stochastic long-… ▽ More Offline goal-conditioned reinforcement learning remains challenging in stochastic long-horizon settings, where compounding value estimation errors hinder reliable goal reaching. While prior methods have sought to address this challenge through various approaches, a key challenge remains path mirage, where agents overcommit to spuriously successful trajectories in offline datasets induced by stochasticity, often leading to failure on difficult tasks. To address this issue, we propose Branch-aware Graph Planning (BGP), which captures stochastic branching structures by identifying high-variability points as graph nodes and designing goal-conditioned edges with segmented learning to avoid unreliable branches and reduce unnecessary stochasticity along paths. As a result, BGP enables reliable goal reaching even in highly stochastic long-horizon environments, and experiments on diverse OGBench tasks show that it substantially outperforms prior state-of-the-art offline goal-conditioned RL methods. Our implementation is available at https://umlic.github.io/papers/BGP/. △ Less

    Heavy-Tailed Memory Traces in Long-Horizon Language Agents

    Authors: Xinyuan Song, Zekun Cai

    Abstract: Long-horizon language agents are becoming increasingly important for tasks that require persistent interaction, planning, and reasoning over extended trajectories. External memory plays a central role in these agents by preserving past states, transitions, and experiences beyond the limited context window. Existing mem… ▽ More Long-horizon language agents are becoming increasingly important for tasks that require persistent interaction, planning, and reasoning over extended trajectories. External memory plays a central role in these agents by preserving past states, transitions, and experiences beyond the limited context window. Existing memory systems, however, are typically evaluated mainly by task success and token cost, with much less attention to how memory usage is distributed across stored information. We show that repeated retrieval can produce heavy-tailed memory usage, where a small set of states is accessed frequently while many others are rarely retrieved and more prone to prediction errors. We study this phenomenon through a systematic tail audit across retrieval policies and graph structures. We find that memory concentration is reproducible but strongly policy-dependent, ranging from log-normal-compatible patterns under random walks to truncated-power-law-compatible heavy tails under semantic LLM policies. Motivated by these findings, we propose Core-Tail Memory Controller (CTMC), a rank-based memory controller that allocates prompt budget with a single exponent $τ$ while preserving summarized information from the tail. Across Synthetic Graph World and ALFWorld, CTMC substantially reduces memory usage while preserving performance, and on LongMemEval it reduces prompt tokens by 24.48% while maintaining aggregate accuracy. These results show that heavy-tailed memory usage is both a measurable property of long-horizon agent memory and a practical signal for improving memory efficiency. △ Less

    cs.AI

    World Model Science: Self-Organized Criticality, Weak Chaos, and Metastable Belief Dynamics in Long-Horizon LLM Agents

    Authors: Xinyuan Song, Zekun Cai

    Abstract: Long-… ▽ More Long-horizon LLM agents must maintain and update a world model over extended sequences of observations, actions, decisions and tool calls. We study the dynamics of long horizon agents from three perspectives: self-organized criticality, weak chaos, and metastable belief dynamics. Through extensive experiments on controlled puzzles, tool-use tasks, embodied environments, multi-hop retrieval, general-assistant reasoning, and Game of Life, we study how world-model errors develop over long interactions. We find that externally increased task pressure can sharply raise the probability of collapse, while naturally occurring pressure is only a modest predictor of later failure. Errors are temporally correlated rather than independent, and their propagation changes with dependency depth while remaining bounded in finite tasks. We also find that locally valid actions can continue after the agent's overall task state has become incorrect, that error structure depends on task topology and horizon, and that belief states in embodied tasks are shallow rather than strongly trapped in incorrect states. Larger horizons allow larger error cascades, but their scale remains constrained by the task and the model's capability. Overall, we observe power-law behavior, bounded divergence consistent with weak chaos, and metastable belief dynamics across long-horizon agent interactions. We call this perspective world model science: the empirical study of self organized criticality in long-horizon agent world models. △ Less

However, existing agents mainly retrieve records or summaries, lacking the ability to form and refine causal understanding of the world. Inspired by human causal cognition, we introduce CauProphet, an agent that develops and updates causal knowledge within its Causal Cognition. For each task, CauProphet combines relevant knowledge in Causal Cognition with current evidence to construct a Causal Belief, a structured belief capturing causal factors and the effects of possible actions. It uses this belief for decision-making and continuously updates both the belief and Causal Cognition as new observations emerge, enabling causal knowledge that persists beyond individual experiences. Evaluations demonstrate that CauProphet progressively acquires causal knowledge and transfers it to future decision-making. In financial research, CauProphet leverages prior discoveries to improve downstream analyses and outperforms all baselines in mean rank correlation with next-day returns, achieving a 25.4% improvement over a baseline agent. It also improves prediction accuracy from 21.3% to 28.6% in dynamic real-world settings. Further experiments validate its ability to perform reliable causal analysis and action selection across scientific and everyday tasks, demonstrating the potential of causal experience learning for next-generation LLM agents. The project repository is available at https://github.com/OpenCausaLab/CauProphet. △ Less

A completed action does not ensure that its effects will persist: a later disturbance can invalidate support that the remaining plan still assumes. We present LOGIV, a graph engineering framework for agentic robot execution that uses an explicit task graph to connect symbolic planning, observation-based verification, and embodied action. LOGIV compiles a validated plan into condition-support and resource-ordering edges, checks their physical validity during execution, and uses dependency tracing to determine what must be restored after a failure. Repair is constrained by retained unfinished actions, and the combined continuation is symbolically validated before execution resumes. The framework operates above existing Vision-Language-Action (VLA) and World Action Model (WAM) policies without adding trainable components. Across 20 dual-arm tasks from RoboTwin~V2 and BiCoord with three execution backbones, LOGIV improves mean task success without injected disturbances by 10.67 and 15.33 percentage points, respectively. Under predefined perturbations, it achieves higher task completion and condition restoration than three adapted recovery baselines for every backbone on both benchmarks. On three real-robot tasks, successful trials increase from 12/30 to 17/30. These results support explicit, continuously checked task dependencies as a practical interface for reliable agentic robot execution. △ Less

We develop randomized techniques for two parts of the offline stage while preserving the Hamiltonian structure of the reduced dynamics. First, we… ▽ More Symplectic model order reduction of Hamiltonian systems yields reduced dynamics that conserve the reduced Hamiltonian along exact trajectories, avoiding the artificial energy drift that can arise from conventional projection methods. For large-scale spatial discretizations, however, basis construction and reduced-operator assembly can involve substantial offline costs. Randomized numerical linear algebra can reduce these costs through low-dimensional sketches while supporting streaming and parallel implementations. We develop randomized techniques for two parts of the offline stage while preserving the Hamiltonian structure of the reduced dynamics. First, we introduce the one-pass sketched randomized cSVD (Ops-rcSVD), a one-pass randomized variant of the complex-SVD algorithm. Combining randomized range finding with an independent left sketch, Ops-rcSVD constructs an ortho-symplectic reduced basis from a single pass over the snapshot ensemble. Second, for quadratic Hamiltonian systems, we introduce a randomized reduced-Hamiltonian assembly based on sketching the reduced basis and operator actions, yielding a reduced Hamiltonian system by construction. We derive bounds for the snapshot projection error, uniform-in-parameter perturbation bounds for the sketched reduced Hamiltonian, and an energy-norm estimate for the difference between reduced and sketched reduced trajectories. Experiments on large-scale systems show that Ops-rcSVD attains projection accuracy comparable to deterministic and two-pass randomized constructions while reducing basis-construction time. They further demonstrate accurate sketched reduced-Hamiltonian assembly when storing the complete snapshot ensemble is no longer feasible. △ Less

    Focused Forcing: Content-Aware Per-Frame KV Selection for Efficient Autoregressive Video Diffusion

    Authors: Peiliang Cai, Evelyn Zhang, Jiacheng Liu, Hao Lin, Ruiqi Zhang, Weile Mo, Yue Ma, Shikang Zheng, Jiehang Huang, Dongrui Liu, Linfeng Zhang

    Abstract: Recent advances in autoregressive video diffusion have enabled sequential and streaming video generation. However, long-horizon generation requires increasingly large KV caches, making efficient compression without sacrificing quality challenging. Existing methods mostly select h… ▽ More Recent advances in autoregressive video diffusion have enabled sequential and streaming video generation. However, long-horizon generation requires increasingly large KV caches, making efficient compression without sacrificing quality challenging. Existing methods mostly select historical frames based on attention scores, but their context decisions remain coarse. When multiple frames are generated in the same chunk, these methods often apply a shared history selection to the whole chunk, score historical frames solely by attention, and assign head-wise budgets either uniformly or by attention-pattern heuristics rather than explicit head-importance estimation. We show that frames within the same generated chunk can depend on distinct historical frames, that the same historical frame can receive different attention scores as its relative temporal distance to the current frames changes, and that masking different heads induces unequal generation degradation. Motivated by these findings, we propose Focused Forcing, a training-free KV selection method that focuses cached history along both generated-frame and head dimensions. For each generated frame, Focused Forcing preserves the most relevant and distinctive historical frames by combining attention scores with diversity scores of historical frames, while assigning larger budgets to heads with higher estimated importance. Across multiple autoregressive generation paradigms, Focused Forcing achieves up to 1.48x end-to-end acceleration without training, while improving visual quality and text alignment. Our code is available at https://github.com/caipeiliang2004/Focused-Forcing. △ Less

    cs.LG cs.CL cs.CR

    Surprise Alone Learns Lies: Evidence-Gated Continual Learning for LLMs from Streaming Claims

    Authors: Abdelkader Metales, Miriam Grabenheinrich

    Abstract: …learning methods for large language models increasingly decide what to learn by surprise, keeping the inputs the model finds least predictable. This rule is unsafe when the stream contains lies. A planted falsehood about something the model knows has low probability under the model's belief, so it is highly surprising by construction, and a surprise-driv… ▽ More Continual learning methods for large language models increasingly decide what to learn by surprise, keeping the inputs the model finds least predictable. This rule is unsafe when the stream contains lies. A planted falsehood about something the model knows has low probability under the model's belief, so it is highly surprising by construction, and a surprise-driven learner absorbs nearly every such lie, at a rate similar to random selection with the same budget. We study the problem with LoRA-adapted students (Qwen2.5-1.5B, Qwen2.5-3B and Llama-3.2-3B) that learn from a stream of true and false claims about invented people and real CounterFact facts. We report four findings. (1) A verifier key, a judge that must approve each claim, removes lies to the extent that its verifier is accurate; real LLM judges are cautious and reject unsourced truths and even source-backed updates. (2) Calibrated regression tests on unrelated knowledge slow learning down without filtering out lies. (3) The original model's own prior flags claims that contradict what it knows, without a judge. It separates lies about known facts from true new facts (AUC 0.78-0.83 across three models) but not from legitimate updates of the same kind of fact (AUC 0.53). It saves 43% of judge calls, but an attacker with access to the model can choose lies that pass it. (4) If trusted sources exist that an attacker cannot plant, routing contradicted claims to evidence recovers legitimate updates and the combined system has the lowest poison rate among learned claims; when an attacker plants matching sources for 30% of the lies, the system ends up worse than the judge alone. The acceptance signals answer different questions, and none of them decides on its own whether a claim is true. △ Less

    GROME: GROunding Factorized MEmory into Video World Models

    Authors: Fang Yu, Xiaoxue Wu, Haiyu Zhang, Yangfu Li, Botong Zhao, Yu Qiao, Tianfan Xue, Yue Lu, Xinyuan Chen

    Abstract: Long-… ▽ More Long-horizon consistency is essential for video world models, yet it becomes difficult to maintain once observed content falls outside the context window. Existing memory mechanisms either retrieve history as implicit context, which remains constrained by context capacity and entangles static and dynamic information or consolidate history into explicit global representations, which struggle to model evolving dynamics and accumulate geometric errors. To overcome these limitations, we introduce \textbf{GROME}, a novel factorized memory framework that augments video world models for long-horizon coherence across both static scenes and dynamic entities. GROME operates via three synergistic components: 1)~\emph{Static-Dynamic Factorization} decouples historical evidence into static and dynamic components, preserving static scene geometry and dynamic entity states separately; 2)~\emph{Local Memory Retrieval} selects spatially aligned static history to eliminate geometric drift alongside the latest dynamic entity states to ensure temporal continuity; 3)~\emph{Training-Free Memory Injection} seamlessly grounds the factorized memory into video world models via their native conditioning pathways. Experiments on long-horizon revisit scenarios demonstrate that GROME substantially enhances scene persistence and dynamic entity fidelity over existing methods, all while preserving the visual quality and controllability of the frozen backbone without additional training. Beyond self-rollouts, GROME readily accommodates external observations as plug-and-play memory, unlocking flexible and controllable video continuation and scene exploration. △ Less

    cs.AR

    Caerus: Prefetching by Thinking Globally and Acting Locally

    Authors: Jacky Wong, Jacob Alexandrou, Yinting Huang, Luke Panayi, Lev Mukhanov, Sam Ainsworth, Paul Kelly

    Abstract: …prefetching, prefetches are issued at an address delta relative to the most recently observed access, selecting deltas that have exhibited high coverage in the recent access-stream history. The Best-Offset Prefetcher (BOP) exemplifies this approach by selecting a single uniformly-issued global offset that delivers timely prefetches. However, its lack of cont… ▽ More Modern processors rely heavily on data prefetching to bridge the growing performance gap between computation and memory accesses. In offset prefetching, prefetches are issued at an address delta relative to the most recently observed access, selecting deltas that have exhibited high coverage in the recent access-stream history. The Best-Offset Prefetcher (BOP) exemplifies this approach by selecting a single uniformly-issued global offset that delivers timely prefetches. However, its lack of context-sensitive accuracy tracking results in significantly increased DRAM traffic and sometimes disappointing performance. Conversely, Berti, the state-of-the-art offset prefetcher, achieves impressive prefetching performance and accuracy. However, its Program-Counter (PC)-local approach to extracting offsets limits its ability to capture global memory patterns, meaning there are many scenarios where simpler global offset prefetchers, such as BOP, outperform it. Observing the limitations of contemporary offset prefetchers, we present Caerus: an offset prefetcher that employs PC-local filtering on multiple non-overlapping globally derived offsets. Caerus extracts multiple offsets from the global memory trace and maximizes their combined coverage by training each offset only on accesses not already covered by previously learned offsets. The accuracy of each extracted offset is tracked per-PC based on whether it can cover future accesses for any PC. This allows Caerus to address complex delta patterns while maintaining high accuracy. In SPECspeed 2017, Caerus gains a geomean speedup of 8.3%, outperforming Berti by 2.0% while achieving low hardware and DRAM-traffic overhead. △ Less

This conversational-training effect is not specific to frame-level decoding. Under a matched complete-or-not test it also appears at the utterance level, across two encoders, but only on conversational test data. Testing only on single-turn speech, as most prior work does, therefore hides the benefit of conversational training data. Existing benchmarks typically evaluate short interactions or rely on camera trajectories fixed before generation. The performance of video world models under long-horizon exploration with extensive camera motion remains under-explored. We introduce WorldProbe, a diagnostic benchmark that uses a goal-conditioned multimodal agent to adapt camera actions online during generation. WorldProbe comprises 164 test cases across four probing families. Across 10 state-of-the-art models, we generate more than 1,600 rollouts totaling approximately 800 minutes, with horizons up to 180 seconds. Our evaluation across four dimensions reveals both the degradation of local measures and the emergence of long-range inconsistencies. We find that recent models sustain visual and content quality for longer, while long-range memory and evolution remain weak. These results expose a gap between locally stable generation and persistent world consistency that is largely missed by existing benchmarks. Code and benchmark resources are available at https://github.com/Untitled-W/WorldProbe. △ Less

Treating time as another spatial dimension allows parallelization across time and simultaneous adaptivity in spatial and temporal discretizations. However, space-time formulations suffer from the curse of dimensionality due to the (d+1)-dimensional domain. Time-series forecasting methods, such as dynamic mode decomposition (DMD), also suffer the same scaling for storing and processing measurement data. In this work, we investigate whether quantum-inspired tensor network methods can mitigate this curse for both solving PDEs and data-driven modeling. We formulate a space-time matrix product state (MPS) encoding to represent either the space-time solution or time-series data. Using this representation, we develop a unified framework that combines an MPS space-time PDE solver with an MPS-DMD algorithm. We benchmark our MPS space-time solver for linear and nonlinear PDEs in both (1+1) and (2+1) dimensions, including non-diffusive regimes such as shockwave formation and Allen-Cahn phase separation. We observe that the MPS ansatz efficiently captures the spatio-temporal correlations of the solutions, with a 99% reduction in parameters with respect to the grid size. We demonstrate the MPS-DMD algorithm in two cases. First, the MPS space-time solver generates the MPS encoding of the time-series data. We then apply MPS-DMD directly to predict the dynamics without solving PDEs. Second, for the general case where data is available as dense arrays, MPS-DMD needs an intermediate compression step. We implement a streaming-like compression algorithm and observe a 97% reduction in memory, with MPS-DMD predictions matching those of exact DMD. △ Less

    Authors: Kiljoon Han, Kyumin Hwang, Wonheok Choi, Jeonghoon Kim, Sunghoom Im

This challenge is magnified in video streams, where traditional online methods resort to reducing feature channels or relying on expensive temporal warping. To address this, we propose \textbf{DEQ-Stereo}, a novel video stereo matching framework based on the Deep Equilibrium (DEQ) paradigm. Instead of unrolling a fixed number of iterations, DEQ-Stereo reformulates the refinement as an implicit fixed-point problem, adaptively terminating once convergence is met. To ensure fast and stable intra-frame convergence, we introduce the \textbf{Convolutional Simplified Gated Unit (ConvSGU)}, which eliminates unnecessary non-linearities by modeling the latent state as a gated, element-wise sum of inputs. Furthermore, to exploit cross-frame temporal coherence, we propose \textbf{Temporal State Transfer (TST)}, a warm-start strategy that propagates the converged hidden state to the next frame without requiring explicit pose or warping priors. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that DEQ-Stereo delivers state-of-the-art performance with a superior accuracy-to-latency trade-off. △ Less

    FutureWorld: A Plug-and-Play Causal Next-State Predictor for Interactive Video World Exploration

    Authors: Guangyuan Li, Kelu Yao, Shuolin Xu, Xinlei Yu, Wei Xing, Lei Zhao, Peng-Tao Jiang, Xiaogang Xu, Xiaobin Hu

    Abstract: …across three benchmarks for interactive long-video generation and action-conditioned world modeling. Extensive experiments demonstrate reduced error accumulation and improved long-horizon consistency across diverse generation settings. The project page is at https://GuangYuanKK.github.io/futureworld. ▽ More Interactive video world models aim to follow user actions while maintaining scene consistency over extended interactions. History-based methods reuse generated latents to preserve scene content, but three limitations of this reuse contribute to error recirculation. 1) The latent generated at each interaction is retained as history, potentially containing blurred details, geometric distortions, or semantic drift. 2) Although retrieved historical content matches the current scene, it can still contain errors that are carried into the next chunk. 3) New outputs then become history for later interactions, potentially reinforcing the same errors through repeated reuse. To limit this cycle, we aim to predict the future scene state from the current generated latent and the next action before generating the next interaction's video chunk. This future-state prior provides guidance for the next chunk, with the aim of reducing the errors carried forward from history. To this end, we propose a plug-and-play Causal Next-Chunk Predictor (C-NCP) with cross-model adaptability and prediction-guided modulation. We integrate C-NCP into four base generators and evaluate the augmented models across three benchmarks for interactive long-video generation and action-conditioned world modeling. Extensive experiments demonstrate reduced error accumulation and improved long-horizon consistency across diverse generation settings. The project page is at https://GuangYuanKK.github.io/futureworld. △ Less

    Improved constraints on the Milky Way potential using the M68 stream and DESI spectroscopic data

    Authors: Carles G. Palau, Wenting Wang, Jiaxin Han, J. Aguilar, S. Ahlen, F. Beutler, D. Bianchi, D. Brooks, A. Carnero Rosell, F. J. Castander, T. Claybaugh, A. Cuceu, A. de la Macorra, J. E. Forero-Romero, E. Gaztañaga, Satya Gontcho A Gontcho, G. Gutierrez, J. Guy, D. Kirkby, A. Kremin, M. Landriau, L. Le Guillou, A. Leauthaud, G. E. Medina, A. Meisner , et al. (19 additional authors not shown)

    Abstract: We present a selection of stars belonging to the stellar stream of the M68 (NGC 4590) globular cluster, also known as Fjörm. This star selection is an improvement on previous ones that used only Gaia data, as it incorporates spectroscopic measurements from the DESI survey and photometric data from the DESI Legacy Surveys. The selection contains 96 stars, eac… ▽ More We present a selection of stars belonging to the stellar stream of the M68 (NGC 4590) globular cluster, also known as Fjörm. This star selection is an improvement on previous ones that used only Gaia data, as it incorporates spectroscopic measurements from the DESI survey and photometric data from the DESI Legacy Surveys. The selection contains 96 stars, each with five phase-space parameters from Gaia-DR3 and radial velocity from DESI, covering the entire observed section of the stream. This constitutes the largest selection of M68 stream stars with measured radial velocities to date. The observed stream is wider than expected from N-body simulations, and the stars farthest from the centre of the stream appear to be correlated in radial velocity space. This suggests that these stars cannot have been stripped from the cluster in a static axisymmetric potential. By modelling a mock sample of stream stars created using an N-body simulation, we found that we could reliably constrain the disc mass $M_{\rm d}$ and the dark matter halo axis ratio $q_{\rm h}$ of the Milky Way. This is because the stream flows close to and almost parallel to the disc. Using the 44 stars that are consistent with having been stripped from the cluster, combined with measurements of the Milky Way's rotation curve, we constrain the Galactic potential, obtaining $M_{\rm d} = 5.34 \pm 0.57 \times 10^{10}$ M$_{\rm sun}$ and an oblate halo of $q_{\rm h} = 0.83^{+0.06}_{-0.05}$. Additionally, by fitting the stream track, we estimate the Heliocentric distance of M68 to be $r=10.55\pm0.09$ kpc. △ Less

    The Cost of Working Memory in Streaming Mean Estimation

    Authors: Zeyu Wu, Zihao Wang, Minghua Deng

    Abstract: A streaming estimator must compute from the evidence that fits in memory. We determine when storing an accurate mean is easier than computing it. For Gaussian mean estimation over the Euclidean unit ball in $\mathbb{R}^d$, we establish the minimax rate under squared error with $K$ persistent states, a known horizon, and a public time index:… ▽ More A streaming estimator must compute from the evidence that fits in memory. We determine when storing an accurate mean is easier than computing it. For Gaussian mean estimation over the Euclidean unit ball in $\mathbb{R}^d$, we establish the minimax rate under squared error with $K$ persistent states, a known horizon, and a public time index: $\min\{1, K^{-2/d}+\frac{d}{n} (1+\frac{\log(ed)}{1+(\log K)/d})\}$. Unrestricted computation followed by $K$-level encoding has rate $\min\{1,d/n+K^{-2/d}\}$. At a fixed sufficiently small error, statistically optimal sample size therefore requires order $d\log d$ working bits, while order $d$ bits suffice to store the answer. The lower bound tracks repeated state compression through an entropy potential obtained by Gaussian smoothing. A matching construction combines a multiscale modular counter with workspace reused across coordinate groups. In one dimension, it attains the terminal rate $n^{-1}+K^{-2}$. Any fixed confidence requirement that the realized loss not exceed the zero baseline preserves these rates. Increasing confidence creates a further memory requirement: at fixed nontrivial risk in every fixed dimension, failure probability $e^{-γn}$ requires and permits order $\log n$ working states for sufficiently small fixed $γ>0$. In the scalar problem, a fixed terminal alphabet suffices under the same protection. These bounds separate the memory needed to represent an answer from the memory needed to accumulate evidence and validate a decision. △ Less

Pure adversarial distillation offers a simpler route, yet has struggled at modern text-to-image scale. To address this limitation, we propose ROAD (Representation-grounded One-step Adversarial Distillation), a one-stage framework that distills text-to-image models with one adversarial objective and a lightweight representation-space critic. First, we replace the classical convolutional head with a Lipschitz-controlled Transformer head with token-level text conditioning, which matches the semantic tokens of modern visual encoders. Second, we observed a fixed random feature mixing leads to long-horizon degradation. We attribute it to generator-critic co-adaptation around one realization-specific view, and periodically re-draw the mixing to keep the randomization effective. Third, for our dense and vector-valued logits, we introduce Jacobian R1 which penalizes the full input-logit Jacobian, overcoming pooled R1's limitation of neglecting most of logit sensitivities. A single adversarial objective then suffices: in one step, ROAD reaches 11.03 FID on SDXL and 13.73 on SD3.5-M on COCO-10K, surpassing the multi-step teachers at a fraction of distribution matching's training compute. △ Less

In practice, artists often inspect an initial rig and repeatedly edit its skeletal structure and deformation behavior to meet specific animation requirements. Existing automatic methods primarily generate a plausible rig from geometry, offering limited explicit control over the resulting skeleton and deformation behavior. In this work, we present ViP-Rig, a visual-prompted framework that supports both prompt-first rigging and result-guided editing by injecting features extracted from user-drawn or edited 2D skeletal and rigidity prompts into frozen pretrained backbones. Specifically, ViP-Rig consists of two stages, Skeleton Generation and Skinning Prediction. In the first stage, the skeletal sketch is processed by the Dense-to-Compact Visual Prompt Encoding to produce compact, fixed-length conditioning tokens. The resulting tokens are injected into a frozen pretrained autoregressive generator through gated adapters to control joint placement and branching structure while preserving the generator's geometric prior. In the second stage, the rigidity map is processed using the same visual encoding design, while the pretrained skinning backbone remains frozen. The resulting tokens are symmetrically injected into the point and joint streams to modulate point-joint compatibility and the resulting skinning weights. Experiments on Articulation-XL2.0 and zero-shot evaluation on ModelsResource show that ViP-Rig more accurately recovers target skeletons and skinning weights than geometry-conditioned baselines under prompt-guided evaluation. Qualitative results further demonstrate explicit and localized control in both prompt-first rigging and result-guided editing. △ Less

    Causality-informed dynamic mode decomposition for unsteady flow analysis

    Authors: Yihua Pan, Jiaqing Kou

    Abstract: …jet. Compared with standard DMD, GC-DMD improves operator identification accuracy in the linear benchmark and improves the robustness of the identified spectrum while reducing long-horizon prediction errors in the cavity flows. For the turbulent jet, it yields a sparser operator with improved prediction accuracy. These… ▽ More Causal analysis characterizes directional dependencies in dynamical systems, while such information is not typically used to inform reduced-order modeling of unsteady flows. This study proposes a Granger Causality-informed Dynamic Mode Decomposition (GC-DMD) framework that incorporates such information into data-driven modeling and modal analysis of high-dimensional flow data. The framework uses conditional Granger causality estimates to regularize the identification of the DMD operator, preferentially suppressing weakly supported interactions while preserving dynamically relevant couplings. This regularization promotes sparse operators informed by directional predictive dependencies, leading to improved predictive accuracy and identification of dynamically relevant modes. GC-DMD is evaluated on a linear system with a known coupling structure, lid-driven cavity flows with varying dynamical complexity, and a broadband transonic turbulent jet. Compared with standard DMD, GC-DMD improves operator identification accuracy in the linear benchmark and improves the robustness of the identified spectrum while reducing long-horizon prediction errors in the cavity flows. For the turbulent jet, it yields a sparser operator with improved prediction accuracy. These results demonstrate the potential of incorporating causality information to guide the identification of sparse, predictive reduced-order models of complex flows. The \href{https://github.com/JiaqingKou/GC-DMD-Granger-Causality-informed-DMD}{source codes} \footnote{https://github.com/JiaqingKou/GC-DMD-Granger-Causality-informed-DMD} from this research are publicly available in the GitHub repository. △ Less

    Authors: Dongyan Sun, Neelesh Gupta, Rajgopal Kannan, Viktor K. Prasanna

    Abstract: Graph analytics generates irregular, topology-dependent memory-access streams via indirect traversals, limiting data prefetching. Accurate prediction enables timely prefetches that hide memory latency. However, ML predictors couple short histories with fixed horizons, fitting the training trace's node layout rather than learning transferable graph patterns. We propose LoCoMAP, a long-context representation-learning framework for memory-access prediction with independently controlled observation history and prediction horizon. We evaluate Connected Components (CC), PageRank (PR), and Single-Source Shortest Paths (SSSP) on six synthetic graph families, with Erdos-Renyi (ER) and Grid as random and deterministic controls. We test two interventions: same-family regeneration changes connectivity, while node shuffling disrupts layout but preserves connectivity. Across 18 graph-workload pairs, extending history from 8 to 128 accesses improves F1 by 7.8%, while extending prediction horizon from 8 to 512 improves F1 by 88.3%. For CC, regeneration is less disruptive than shuffling: without retraining, long context retains 95.4% of original F1 and outperforms short context in six families. Under full shuffle, long context improves CC F1 by 33.6% across ER, Barabasi-Albert, Copying Model, and Stochastic Block Model (SBM), with a 2.06x gain on SBM, but not on Watts-Strogatz or Grid, where near-zero delta mass shifts into heavy tails beyond the predictor's range. Long context outperforms short context on six real-world CC graphs. At full shuffle, long-context PR retains 57.6% of original F1 and averages 11.0% above short context. Both SSSP models retain about 30% of original F1 under full shuffle as distance updates access vertex data in a new order. Within these bounds, long-context predictors are robust across related graphs and layouts without retraining, enabling a generalizable graph prefetcher. △ Less

    Authors: Prashant Chaudhary, Kapil Khandelwal

    Abstract: An ad break in a live stream is a hard deadline. Comparing trigger policies means scoring which of these happened; the records easiest to collect do not. We separate tri… ▽ More An ad break in a live stream is a hard deadline. Once a cue arrives, the ad path has seconds to produce a state the player can use, and starting that work earlier buys time while giving the prepared state longer to expire before the break. Comparing trigger policies means scoring which of these happened; the records easiest to collect do not. We separate trigger, reach, readiness, playback, billability and measurement in an observed-event ledger, fix the reference-deadline rule before candidates are assigned, and account for contribution over every planned break-session pair, reached or not. Readiness means the declared state is valid at that deadline. We prove when scoring completion by the deadline instead reverses a policy comparison: for monotone playback with a shared latency law, a fixed state lifetime and common coefficients, the reversal occurs when the earlier policy's extra trigger cost falls between its validity gain and its completion gain. A synthetic study of 45,000 scripted sessions matches this condition on monotone paths, a check of its generator, and, outside its assumptions, finds the same reversal in mixtures with restarts, seeks, late joins and pauses. Playhead summaries misclassify up to 41% of reach indicators yet reverse no comparison in the study: maximum-position errors cancel exactly, and terminal-position errors change only magnitudes. Completion scoring reverses the comparison in three of nine short-lifetime conditions, all at the one latency setting where the proved condition holds. The evidence is synthetic by design; code, seeds and event histories are public. △ Less

    Authors: Yichuan Chen, Xin Wang

    Abstract: …at every turn whether anything in its store is worth an interruption. We show that this decision is a multiple-testing problem whose difficulty grows with the store size N. On streaming replays of LongMemEval, a similarity threshold tuned to 5% false alarms at N=8 reaches 84-100% at N=2048 across embedding models and cue types, and LoCoMo and LLM judges show… ▽ More A long-lived assistant that brings up memories without being asked must decide at every turn whether anything in its store is worth an interruption. We show that this decision is a multiple-testing problem whose difficulty grows with the store size N. On streaming replays of LongMemEval, a similarity threshold tuned to 5% false alarms at N=8 reaches 84-100% at N=2048 across embedding models and cue types, and LoCoMo and LLM judges show it too. We prove that a gate which thresholds the best of N independent irrelevant scores at false-alarm rate alpha operates at a per-item false-positive rate of about -ln(1-alpha)/N, which pins its hit rate to the far left of the single-comparison ROC curve and, under regular variation, makes it regularly varying in N. A finite-pool form of this bound predicts the hit rate of a label-tuned threshold on held-out questions to within 6.1 points. At matched false alarms the deployed score recalls 14-26 points more than raw cosine. A cue-swap conformal gate, which ranks the current cue among other cues on the same store, has a finite-sample false-alarm guarantee under exchangeability. With a background drawn from the user's unfiltered history it keeps false alarms at 3-6% on LongMemEval at the hit rate of a label-tuned threshold; on LoCoMo it overshoots or turns conservative depending on where its reference cues come from. In a chronological replay with the user's own earlier messages as references, it speaks on 6-8% of session-opening turns, against 57% for the deployed gate at N=2048, at about 50 ms per decision on one CPU thread. A cross-encoder reranker pays the same price of scale, and on a prospective-memory benchmark, similarity gates recall at most 9% of constraints at controlled false alarms. One human and two LLM annotators judge most false alarms unhelpful for a response. Code and data: https://github.com/RiverChenStillCompiling/price-of-scale △ Less
