    Emergence of an acoustic black hole in a lattice model

    Authors: Antonino Flachi

    Abstract: We build an acoustic black hole in a lattice model: a draining vortex evolved by the local collide-and-stream rule of a lattice-Boltzmann model, with no acoustic metric imposed, the geometry being measured from the steady state. A horizon emerges, first located from the measured Mach profile and then confirmed by phonon ray tracing on the measured metric, an… ▽ More We build an acoustic black hole in a lattice model: a draining vortex evolved by the local collide-and-stream rule of a lattice-Boltzmann model, with no acoustic metric imposed, the geometry being measured from the steady state. A horizon emerges, first located from the measured Mach profile and then confirmed by phonon ray tracing on the measured metric, and the ringdown after a perturbation follows approximately the quasinormal frequencies of the draining bathtub. The residual departures are traced to the cell-scale structure of the horizon, which is not an ideal absorber. We model it in two ways: as a reflective horizon on the ideal bathtub, with two free parameters (the modulus and phase of the reflectivity), and as the sonic point of the realised flow absorbing in its own frame, with no adjustable parameter. The second describes the horizon and the non-rotating spectrum best. Superradiance has two ingredients: the emergent geometry, which amplifies in a loss-free calculation on the measured metric, and the horizon, whose absorption frame sets the width and strength of the amplified band but leaves no trace in the ringdown. Both models are used to examine superradiance, which has not been observed in the present toy model: whether the lattice hole superradiates dynamically remains open. The lattice leaves its own imprint as well: a fourfold anisotropy of the sonic surface and a surface gravity set by the cell-scale structure of the horizon. △ Less

    Try Before You Evolve: Ranking-Scored Trials for Recursive Self-Improvement of Recommender Systems

    Authors: Xu Huang, Jiawei Feng, Jiancan Wu

    Abstract: Recommenders on Web platforms such as e-commerce and music streaming need their training recipes re-tuned for each dataset. Large language model (LLM) agents can now rewrite these recipes, enabling recursive self-improvement (RSI) of recommender systems. Existing agents propose one edit per round, fully retrain the recommender, and keep the edit only if its… ▽ More Recommenders on Web platforms such as e-commerce and music streaming need their training recipes re-tuned for each dataset. Large language model (LLM) agents can now rewrite these recipes, enabling recursive self-improvement (RSI) of recommender systems. Existing agents propose one edit per round, fully retrain the recommender, and keep the edit only if its ranking quality improves. Unlike programs or patches that can be scored by execution, a recommender edit changes how the model is trained, which raises two challenges. First, ranking feedback is delayed: an edit's ranking quality is known only after full retraining, so much of the retraining budget goes to edits that are later discarded. Second, the signals available before retraining are unreliable: sampled training losses can look solved while full-ranking performance remains poor, and they are not comparable across edits that change the loss or sampler. We propose RecRSI, which generates diverse candidate edits from a diagnosis of the current model and selects one for full retraining using early ranking feedback: each candidate receives a short trial, the first steps of its full training run, scored by full-ranking NDCG@10 on held-out probe users. With two sequential recommenders on three datasets and the same number of full retrainings, RecRSI significantly outperforms existing self-evolving agents in every setting, and on both Amazon datasets reaches in its first round a ranking quality that none of them reaches in five. △ Less

    Breadcrumbing Search Agents: Per-Turn Scheming Over Long-Horizon Trajectories

    Authors: Xuebin Li, Hanqing Zhao, Siyuan Liang, Kejiang Chen, Weiming Zhang, Dacheng Tao, Nenghai Yu

    Abstract: …result per query can substantially increase attack success when the evidence is coordinated across the agent's trajectory. Prior work on search-agent safety primarily focuses on static web-content injection, but modern agents issue follow-up queries and cross-check competing sources, so a single injected page is often diluted or rejected. We show that the channel delivering search and page observations is a fragile security boundary: beyond exposing the agent to a single poisoned page, a mediated search interface can repeatedly steer how the agent gathers evidence and forms its final answer. Under a constrained tool-intermediary threat model, appending only one controlled result per query can substantially increase attack success when the evidence is coordinated across the agent's trajectory. We study this setting with a strategy-driven long-horizon attack system and introduce Authority-Chain Hijack (ACH), an expert-refined strategy that turns isolated search-result and page-content manipulations into a coherent evidence chain across seemingly corroborating sources. ACH achieves the highest Overall ASR among all baselines, reaching 55.9% / 83.3% ASR / MaxN ASR on the full SafeSearch test split. We further introduce Trace-Guided Strategy Evolution (TGSE), which automatically improves attacker strategies from execution traces, replacing manual redesign with trace-driven refinement; its strongest single setting reaches 71.4% / 95.0% in held-out evaluation. Code is available at https://github.com/MBOSSLESS/Breadcrumbing-Search-Agents. △ Less

    VLA-Corrector: Lightweight Detect-and-Correct Inference for Adaptive Action Horizon

    Authors: Yi Pan, Miao Pan, Qi Lu, Jiaming Huang, Man Zhang, Siteng Huang, Xin Li, Jie Zhang, Yongliang Shen, Xuhong Zhang, Wenqi Zhang

    Abstract: …replanning via Online Gradient Guidance (OGG). The detect-and-correct mechanism of VLA-Corrector naturally induces an event-triggered adaptive action horizon: it preserves long-… ▽ More Vision-Language-Action (VLA) foundation models have recently achieved strong progress in embodied intelligence. To reduce policy-call frequency while preserving temporal coherence, most generative policies adopt an action chunk mechanism, executing multiple future actions in an open-loop manner under a fixed action horizon. However, this "predict-then-blindly-execute" paradigm sacrifices closed-loop reactivity: in contact-rich physical interactions, even small local perturbations can rapidly amplify within the open-loop blind spot, leading to compounding errors and ultimately task failure. To address this limitation, we propose VLA-Corrector, a lightweight corrective inference framework for action-chunked VLA policies. Without modifying the backbone policy weights, VLA-Corrector introduces a lightweight Latent-space Vision Monitor (LVM) that continuously compares predicted and actual visual feature evolution, enabling online detection of visual dynamics deviations. Once persistent deviation is detected, the system triggers a truncation event, discards the remaining stale actions, and invokes corrective replanning via Online Gradient Guidance (OGG). The detect-and-correct mechanism of VLA-Corrector naturally induces an event-triggered adaptive action horizon: it preserves long-horizon execution when the current chunk remains reliable, and invokes short-horizon corrective replanning when execution begins to drift. In doing so, VLA-Corrector mitigates the trade-off imposed by static horizons between execution robustness and policy-call frequency. It can be integrated into different VLA models without further retraining the VLA backbone, interrupting compounding errors while preserving much of the efficiency benefit of action chunking and substantially improving robustness in long-horizon, contact-rich robotic manipulation tasks. △ Less

    Efficient Compilation for Hamiltonian Simulation via Global Binary Symplectic Form Simplification

    Authors: Zhaohui Yang, Yuwei Han, Ruiyun Zhang, Dawei Ding, Jianxin Chen, Yuan Feng, Yuan Xie

    Abstract: …exploits simultaneous simplification and commutativity opportunities, achieving efficient global optimization without relying on computationally expensive heuristics or long-horizon searches. Across the generic Hamiltonian simulation benchmarks in HamLib, Symphony achieves average reductions of 59% in two-qubit gate co… ▽ More Hamiltonian simulation is a core quantum workload, underpinning variational quantum algorithms and Trotterized time evolution. Such programs are expressed as Pauli exponential sequences, exhibiting structural patterns that are highly amenable to high-level synthesis and optimization. Existing compilers, however, fail to fully unlock the optimization potential of their global algebraic structure, even when employing advanced graph- or tableau-based methods. We present Symphony, a holistic compilation approach built on the binary symplectic form (BSF) representation of Pauli strings. Unlike prior group-wise BSF simplification and path-based Pauli network synthesis, Symphony applies generalized controlled-Pauli Clifford transformations directly to a global BSF tableau, progressively reducing active Pauli rows while adaptively emitting eligible two-qubit blocks in addition to single-qubit rotations in a forward Clifford frame. Following algebraic simplification, Symphony performs a causality-preserving block rescheduling heuristic that respects frame-induced dependencies while exposing extensive two-qubit block parallelism opportunities. This streamlined compilation style comprehensively exploits simultaneous simplification and commutativity opportunities, achieving efficient global optimization without relying on computationally expensive heuristics or long-horizon searches. Across the generic Hamiltonian simulation benchmarks in HamLib, Symphony achieves average reductions of 59% in two-qubit gate count and 91% in circuit depth. It Pareto-dominates prior state-of-the-art compilers, requiring 1.17--1.58$\times$ fewer two-qubit gates and especially shrinking two-qubit circuit depth by a substantial factor of 1.87--5.67$\times$ on average. △ Less

    Unlimited OCR Works 2

    Authors: Shu Wei, Jingjing Wu, Qunyi Xie, Lingshu Zhang, Xiangcheng Du, Yannian Fu, Mengjie Wang, Zuyi Wang, Zhichao Liu, Yangliu Xu, YY, Yi Yang

However, as document length scales to hundreds of pages, the visual context grows substantially, sharply increasing the computational cost of each decoding step and limiting the scalability of ultra-long document parsing. Inspired by human book transcription via localized focus without re-scanning preceding pages, we introduce Unlimited OCR 2, a document parsing model that looks only at what it transcribes. Building on Unlimited OCR, we upgrade all the decoder attention modules to Sparse Reference Sliding Window Attention (SR-SWA), bounding active reference tokens to a fixed capacity and thus sustaining constant computational costs across arbitrarily long documents. By combining SR-SWA with Unlimited-Horizon Decoding Paradigm, Unlimited OCR 2 enables document parsing over virtually unlimited lengths without prohibitive memory overhead. At the hundred-page scale, Unlimited OCR 2 achieves 7.0$\times$ and 5.6$\times$ per-step speedups over Full Attention and R-SWA, respectively. Furthermore, it delivers state-of-the-art end-to-end performance on both MPDocBench and MarathonDocBench, our newly proposed benchmark targeting hundred-page document parsing. Ultimately, the capacity for uninterrupted, one-shot document parsing signifies a fundamental paradigm shift into the OCR 3.0 era. △ Less

    cs.IR

    PFA: Attention Can Be Progressively Focused in Long-Sequence Generative Recommendation

    Authors: Pengjie Zhou, Weiqin Yang, Haibo Xing, Hao Deng, Jinxin Hu

    Abstract: …recommendation (GR), but computing full attention over long histories during autoregressive beam search decoding incurs substantial online inference latency. To accelerate long-… ▽ More Long user behavioral sequences improve the performance of generative recommendation (GR), but computing full attention over long histories during autoregressive beam search decoding incurs substantial online inference latency. To accelerate long-sequence decoding, existing approaches typically rely on static history compression or dynamic top-k sparse attention; however, the former risks losing critical semantics, whereas the latter incurs significant overhead from repeated token selection and non-contiguous memory access. In this work, we thoroughly analyze attention patterns during GR decoding and reveal two key properties: (i) progressive attention focusing, where attention progressively focuses on a small subset of critical historical tokens; and (ii) cross-step ranking consistency, where their importance rankings remain relatively stable across subsequent decoding steps. Motivated by these observations, we propose Progressively Focused Attention (PFA), an efficient sparse decoding framework for long-sequence GR. PFA retains full attention at the initial decoding step to explore global historical preferences. It then establishes a shared importance ranking through cross-beam weighted aggregation and reorders the key-value cache of the selected tokens into a contiguous memory layout. Subsequent decoding steps reuse this ranking and geometrically reduce the token budget, preserving critical semantics while lowering computational and memory access overhead. Extensive experiments on two public benchmarks and a large-scale industrial dataset demonstrate that PFA outperforms state-of-the-art baselines. A 7-day online A/B test on a large-scale e-commerce advertising platform further confirms that PFA delivers substantial business gains while maintaining low inference latency. Our code is publicly available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/PFA-review-088D. △ Less

    Authors: Daniel Ogenrwot, Jorge Gonzalo Delgado Cervantes, Richard Sserunjogi, John Businge

    Abstract: …library and command-line tool that separates language-specific lexical processing from reusable analyses: each language declares its lexical surface, which yields a lossless stream of typed tokens, and every analysis is implemented once on that stream. Assembling them from specialized tools introduces differences in language coverage, measurement conventions, and workflows. We present PYREPRISM, an open-source Python library and command-line tool that separates language-specific lexical processing from reusable analyses: each language declares its lexical surface, which yields a lossless stream of typed tokens, and every analysis is implemented once on that stream. Across 187 built-in languages, language-specific declarations have a median length of 16 lines, and no language overrides a shared analysis. Against a reference parser, comment and string classification reach F1 scores of 0.994 and 0.958 on 1,412 files in 12 languages that were never used to develop the tokenizer. In a study of a polyglot repository, one tool replaces three; with matched inputs and analysis operations, it takes 2.9s instead of 8.7s on one core. We also characterize where lexical analysis falls short, in complexity estimation and clone screening. Tool: https://github.com/unlv-evol/PyReprism Video: https://youtu.be/IXc-NP5t4GU △ Less

    Mamba Hawkes Process for Event Sequence Modeling

    Authors: Shan Dai, Yuyang Shen, Yuyang Liang, Chenhao Ma, Anningzhe Gao

    Abstract: …experiments on synthetic and realworld datasets demonstrate that MHP and A-MHP consistently outperform state-of-the-art baselines in event prediction tasks, particularly in long-sequence scenarios. Our work establishes a scalable and theoretically grounded paradigm for event sequence modeling, with practical implicatio… ▽ More Modeling asynchronous event sequences is crucial in numerous real-world applications such as healthcare monitoring, financial transaction analysis, and so on. Traditional temporal point processes, including Hawkes Processes, often fail to capture complex dependencies due to their parametric limitations. While neural approaches like RNNs and Transformers have improved flexibility, they struggle with computational inefficiency, and attention saturation. In this paper, we introduce the Mamba Hawkes Process (MHP), the first framework to integrate selective state space model (Mamba) with temporal point processes. MHP leverages time-varying state transitions and input-dependent gating to efficiently encode event history and capture long-term dependencies with linear complexity. Importantly, we provide theoretical guarantees showing that MHP generalizes both classical multi-exponential Hawkes processes and exponential-decay gated RNNs, underscoring its expressive power and theoretical soundness. To address the inherent constraints of pure state space models in handling heterogeneous event interactions, we further develop Adaptive Mamba Hawkes Process (A-MHP) that incorporates two novel mechanisms: a Time-Scaling Mechanism that adaptively weights time intervals based on event type and history, and a Dual-Channel State Transition that adaptively processes event content and temporal dynamics for more refined state updates. Extensive experiments on synthetic and realworld datasets demonstrate that MHP and A-MHP consistently outperform state-of-the-art baselines in event prediction tasks, particularly in long-sequence scenarios. Our work establishes a scalable and theoretically grounded paradigm for event sequence modeling, with practical implications for predictive maintenance, anomaly detection, and dynamic system analysis. △ Less

    Goal-Aware Dynamic Multi-Agent Collaboration for Long-Horizon Tasks

    Authors: Xu Shen, Lingyong Yan, Shuaiqiang Wang, Jianmin Wu, Zhikai Chen, Jiliang Tang, Jie Ren

    Abstract: LLM-based agents are increasingly used to complete complex, long-… ▽ More LLM-based agents are increasingly used to complete complex, long-horizon tasks without human intervention. To make progress toward the task goal, agents need to perform different duties, such as planning and verification, when needed. To understand how single agents manage these duties during long-horizon execution, we conduct a preliminary study. Our analysis shows that verification has the strongest association with task success, but often occurs late, leaving limited opportunities for correction. We also find that the proportion of duty-relevant information decreases as records from different duties accumulate in a shared context. These findings lead us to examine whether multi-agent systems (MAS) can address these problems by assigning duties to agents with separate contexts. Our analysis shows that this separation alone is insufficient. Agents can lose track of the overall goal and repeat prior investigations. They may also lack necessary background while receiving irrelevant information from others. To maintain goal awareness and coordinate collaboration as task needs change, we propose Goal-Aware Dynamic Multi-Agent Collaboration (GAD). It uses a growing execution graph to organize subtasks and their dependencies. To keep agents focused on their goals, each context retains the original instruction and the assigned task's goal and acceptance conditions. To adapt collaboration and information sharing during execution, agents can divide tasks into subtasks and specify dependencies based on execution evidence. These dependencies guide when agents work and which results they receive. Verification and recovery check subtask results and full task completion against their goals, providing feedback for repair when checks fail. △ Less

    Abstract: Long-sequence recommendation can improve along two axes: \emph{evidence scaling} exposes longer histories, while \emph{reasoning scaling} adds layers that interpret that evidence for a candidate. Existing models couple them: sequence encoders propagate every added event through depth, whereas one-shot selection prevents later layers from revisiting omitted evidence. We introduce the Latent Workspace Ranker (LWR), which keeps typed evidence in persistent banks and propagates a fixed-size candidate-local workspace. We compare four architectures across various history--depth configurations using evidence scaling at fixed depth, reasoning scaling at fixed 10K history, and their joint compute-efficient frontier. LWR leads all families at long histories, continues improving with reasoning compute, and converts additional training compute into quality gains at the fastest fitted rate on the joint frontier. At 10K history, it improves NE by 0.19% over HSTU while using 69.6% fewer forward GFLOP/request. It also outperforms existing baselines on two billion-scale ranking surfaces. These results show that separating addressable evidence from propagated state makes reading more and reasoning deeper independently controllable and jointly useful. △ Less

    Authors: Prashant Chaudhary

To measure how far open-source VAST clients meet the player duties of VAST 4.3, whether verdicts… ▽ More Context. Video ads reach streaming and connected-TV apps through VAST documents, and companies count ads from the HTTP requests the player's client library sends. A tracking error can leave playback intact and surface later as disagreeing counts. Objective. To measure how far open-source VAST clients meet the player duties of VAST 4.3, whether verdicts depend on the runtime, what generated inputs add, and when deviations were introduced. Method. A catalog of 108 requirements was extracted from the specification. The open-source clients have 78 findings and 3 to 21 must-level deviations per configuration. None of the tested open-source clients that follow redirect (Wrapper) chains fills the required macros in a Wrapper's request; the archived IMA 3.794.0 configuration tested does. One library's timeout throws an exception no caller can catch, ending a Node.js process. In a pod (ads played back to back), one player credits a finishing ad's completion to the next ad, fixed in release 5.3.0. A library can conform under jsdom and deviate in every tested Chrome- and Safari-family browser. Conclusions. The tested clients diverge on the duties other companies use to count and diagnose ads. A shared conformance suite run in real browsers could expose most graded deviations; the harness and vectors are released. △ Less

We decompose the cancellation by removing the previous layer's write fr… ▽ More In transformer language models with gated multilayer perceptrons (MLPs), adjacent layers write partly against each other into the residual stream. Previous work interpreted this cancellation as layers proposing features and the next layer selectively rejecting the inappropriate ones. We decompose the cancellation by removing the previous layer's write from the input of the next MLP: what the next layer writes along that write splits into what it writes anyway and how much less its MLP writes because the write is present, which we call the damping, and we trace the damping to the neurons that carry it. In gated MLPs the damping is carried by neurons whose gates are closed. SiLU and GELU are slightly negative below zero, so a closed gate still passes a small negative multiple of what the neuron reads, and a neuron whose output weight points along its input weight then subtracts a fraction of the direction it read. Summed over such neurons, closed gates contract the average direction a gated MLP reads, and the previous layer's write is not singled out: the same layer's write at another token is damped as much or more, and the eigenvalue signature from which that work concluded that the correction is selective appears for another token's write as well. Over training the damping grows while what the next layer writes anyway stays flat, and cancellation begins when the damping overtakes it; annealing the negative side of the gate activation away in continued pretraining removes the damping and the cancellation. Adjacent-layer cancellation is therefore weak evidence about which features a model rejects: it is produced by a contraction that gated MLPs apply, on average over directions, to whatever they read. △ Less

Long-… ▽ More Balatro, a 2024 roguelike deckbuilder, presents three difficulties that have historically derailed reinforcement learning methods: a 3,000-action state-dependent action space, horizons of several hundred decisions with sparse rewards, and pervasive stochasticity in both card draws and shop generation. Published RL results on the game remain scarce. We introduce an end-to-end pipeline comprising (i) a high-throughput Rust simulator that is automatically state-diffed against the live game client via BalatroBot, (ii) a condensed observation-action interface with dynamic per-step masking of legal moves, and (iii) a multi-agent PPO architecture that decomposes play into a tactical blind policy and a strategic shop policy that share the full game state but act in disjoint phases. Long-horizon credit assignment is partially addressed by per-agent reward routing with explicit cross-agent credit. On the main joint run, training metrics show a sharp phase transition in which the first-blind clear rate converges above 99% and mean episode return increases by roughly an order of magnitude, plateauing at a mean maximum ante of 3 out of 8; single-agent baselines under identical rewards oscillated between greedy and hoarding failure modes and did not stably clear the first blind. These results establish the two-policy decomposition as a strong reactive baseline for Balatro and localise the remaining gap to long-horizon strategic planning rather than tactical blind play. △ Less

    Multimodal Filesystem Memory For Long-Horizon Embodied Agents

    Authors: Chloe Su, Jiahan Li, Zhenting Qi, Chaoran Cheng, Haoyu Chen, Sejoon Jun, Weirui Ye, Yilun Du

    Abstract: Agents operating over long… ▽ More Agents operating over long horizons need an effective memory that allows them to reuse their experience throughout long interactions. If the entire history of an interaction is retained, it becomes difficult to find the relevant experience, while compressing it into summaries may result in the loss of visual and spatial details that are required later on. We present \textbf{MFM}, a multimodal filesystem memory architecture that keeps complementary types of agent memory. \textbf{Episodic memory} stores the observations, actions, and outcomes, and \textbf{spatial memory} associates experiences with locations. \textbf{Semantic and procedural memory} build up general knowledge and reusable methods of acting, and \textbf{working memory} holds the current plans. MFM makes use of searchable textual records to connect these different representations to the underlying multimodal episodes. The agents are able to find previous experiences by means of contextual cues and to examine the original observations in order to recover details that are missing from their textual descriptions. Through consolidation, the agents convert experience into higher-level knowledge and procedures while still keeping references to the original episodes. In the case of long-horizon Minecraft and computer-use tasks, MFM leads to improved performance with three base models. With GPT-6-Astra, MFM completes \emph{Mine Diamond from Scratch} and reaches 16 out of 24 milestones on the \emph{Ender Dragon} task, as against 12.7 for the best baseline. On CUABench-KiCad it attains a mean reward of 0.440, as compared to 0.400 for the best baseline and 0.267 in the case of no persistent memory. △ Less

    A Spectral Perspective on Stochastic Control Barrier Functions

    Authors: Inkyu Jang, Chams E. Mballo, Claire J. Tomlin, H. Jin Kim

    Abstract: …framework for systems subject to stochastic disturbances by bounding the probability of remaining within a safe set. However, finding a valid SCBF that explicitly reflects the long-horizon behavior of the true safety probability remains a challenge. This paper addresses this issue by adopting a spectral perspective, ut… ▽ More Stochastic control barrier functions (SCBFs) provide a safety-critical control framework for systems subject to stochastic disturbances by bounding the probability of remaining within a safe set. However, finding a valid SCBF that explicitly reflects the long-horizon behavior of the true safety probability remains a challenge. This paper addresses this issue by adopting a spectral perspective, utilizing the linear operator that governs the evolution of the closed-loop system's safety probability. We find that the dominant eigenpair of this Koopman-like operator encodes fundamental safety information of the stochastic system. The dominant eigenfunction is a natural and valid SCBF, with values that characterize relative asymptotic safety across initial states, while the dominant eigenvalue indicates the global asymptotic rate at which the safety probability decays. A practical algorithm for computation is proposed, termed power-policy iteration, which jointly estimates the dominant eigenpair while updating the backup policy with the aim of improving long-term safety. The method is validated using simulation experiments on safety-critical dynamics models. △ Less

Although undoubtedly accreted, BLISS 1 does not exhibit a clear dynamical association with any known progenitor, lying in a high energy (E) - high vertical angular momentum (LZ ) prograde orbit reaching a maximum height from the Galactic plane of zmax ~ 8 kpc. Its chemical properties seem to favor an in situ origin, which might be connected with the Aleph sub-structure.     cs.DS cs.CG

    Dimension Reduction and Coresets for Min-Sum $k$-Clustering

    Authors: Asaf Dallal, Robert Krauthgamer

    Abstract: …, and can be constructed in randomized near-linear-time. We further use these coresets to design streaming algorithms for the problem. ▽ More The min-sum $k$-clustering problem asks to partition an input of $n$ points into $k$ clusters so as to minimize the total pairwise distance within clusters. It is a natural alternative to center-based objectives when the data is not well captured by a small set of representative points. We study for this problem two standard sketching methods, dimension reduction and coresets, which represent the input more compactly while preserving the min-sum cost of every $k$-partition up to $1\pm\varepsilon$ factor. Our first contribution addresses dimension reduction in Euclidean space. The Johnson-Lindenstrauss (JL) Lemma readily implies that a random linear map to target dimension $t=O(\varepsilon^{-2}\log n)$ preserves, with high probability, the min-sum cost of all $k$-partitions. We show that target dimension $t=O(\varepsilon^{-2}(ddim\cdot\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log k+\log\log n))$ suffices for Euclidean inputs of doubling dimension at most $ddim$, and moreover this bound is nearly optimal. We also show that target dimension $t=O(\varepsilon^{-2}(\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log k+\logΔ))$ suffices for Euclidean inputs of aspect ratio at most $Δ$. Our second contribution is to design the first $\varepsilon$-coreset for metric min-sum $k$-clustering. It is actually a movement-based coreset, has size $(1/\varepsilon)^{O(ddim)} k\log n$ for inputs of doubling dimension at most $ddim$, and can be constructed in randomized near-linear-time. We further use these coresets to design streaming algorithms for the problem. △ Less

Together, our training and evaluation recipes provide a path toward more capable and aligned (co-)research agents. △ Less

    Where You Inject Matters: The Trust Region Effect in LLM Agents

    Authors: Chengquan Guo, Xiaodong Liu, Weiwei Yang, Mingqian Feng, Jialin Song, Qiyao Ma, Carolyn Buractaon, Christopher White, Bo Li, Jianfeng Gao

    Abstract: LLM agents are increasingly adopted in long-horizon tasks and interact with diverse external environments. Despite their growing capabilities, these agents remain vulnerable to real-world attacks such as indirect prompt injections, which manipulate agents through malicious instructions embedded in external environments… ▽ More LLM agents are increasingly adopted in long-horizon tasks and interact with diverse external environments. Despite their growing capabilities, these agents remain vulnerable to real-world attacks such as indirect prompt injections, which manipulate agents through malicious instructions embedded in external environments. While existing risk assessment work has primarily focused on the injection content, the effect of where an injection appears has not been systematically characterized. Studying injection placement requires agents to operate over longer-horizon tasks and interact with diverse environmental surfaces. Thus, we build three agentic testbeds spanning tool use, coding, and daily workflow domains with diverse injection surfaces. Holding the injection content fixed, we find that agent susceptibility varies across injection placements. We refer to this phenomenon as the trust region effect: agents implicitly assign different levels of trust to different regions of their environments. For example, an agent powered by GPT-5.4 follows an unsafe injection instruction placed in a repository documentation file (i.e., a README or TODO file) 2.6 times more often on average than the same instruction placed in a code or data file. Building upon this observation, we develop learning-based attackers that generate injection content and select better injection placements. Our approach demonstrates that both where an injection is placed and what content it contains can affect the strength of indirect prompt injection attacks. To the best of our knowledge, this work provides the first systematic characterization for the effectiveness of different injection placements, spanning both coarse-grained environmental surfaces and fine-grained positions. The uneven trust that agents implicitly place across their environments provides important insights for future attack and defense design. △ Less

    Authors: Francisco Tello-Ortiz

    Abstract: …(generalized Vaidya), the gap bounds the blueshift: the advanced time spent in the gapped region is finite, whereas it diverges at a Cauchy horizon. (iii) For counter-streaming null dust, the radiative part $μ$ of the Misner-Sharp mass obeys exactly $\partial_u\partial_vμ=2\,\partial_uμ\,\partial_vμ/(r|f|)$, of which the DTR relation is the thin-shell form;… ▽ More Black-hole interiors with an inner (Cauchy) horizon are unstable through the Poisson-Israel-Ori mass-inflation mechanism, governed by the Dray-'t Hooft-Redmount relation and widely regarded as a dynamical realization of strong cosmic censorship. We show that static, spherically symmetric anisotropic interiors supported by a marginally divergent but integrable density $ρ\sim A/r^2$, with $A>1/(8π)$ and mild screening at finite radius, are incompatible with this mechanism. The compactness $q=m/r$ decreases monotonically from a super-critical central value to $1/2$ at the unique event horizon, producing a strictly positive compactness gap throughout the trapped region and excluding any second zero of the lapse. This gap enters the dynamics quantitatively. (i) Under the null energy condition and a dynamical screening condition, $2m/r$ is non-decreasing along both future null directions of a trapped domain, so the gap propagates under nonlinear spherically symmetric evolution. (ii) For ingoing radiation (generalized Vaidya), the gap bounds the blueshift: the advanced time spent in the gapped region is finite, whereas it diverges at a Cauchy horizon. (iii) For counter-streaming null dust, the radiative part $μ$ of the Misner-Sharp mass obeys exactly $\partial_u\partial_vμ=2\,\partial_uμ\,\partial_vμ/(r|f|)$, of which the DTR relation is the thin-shell form; the in-out coupling $2/(r|f|)$ is uniformly bounded on the interior precisely when the gap is positive. This yields an a priori mass bound and, in the Ori thin-shell model, an e-folding rate bounded by the influx luminosity instead of tending to the inner-horizon surface gravity. Integrable-singularity interiors thus realize a class of sources for which the Poisson-Israel-Ori amplification channel is structurally inoperative in the models in which mass inflation was originally established. △ Less

    Routed Continual Learning

    Authors: Michael Philip, Mahesh Balakrishnan, David Geraghty, David Mao, Vidhya Venkat, Ilya Mironov

    Abstract: …cluster summaries. We also introduce Context Memory Adapter, which mediates between the memory system and the agentic loop. Across four language models and five matched rollouts, Routed Continual Lear… ▽ More Agents deployed at scale repeatedly encounter related problems, and it is natural to let them learn from each other's experience rather than starting over each time. Unfortunately, there is no single way to retain that experience that works well across settings, and no way to know in advance which future problems a given experience will help with. A global history preserves every detail but becomes expensive and mixes together experience that may not belong together; compressing that history into one fixed representation limits the cost but can throw away valuable details. We introduce Routed Continual Learning (Routed CL), an architecture that decides how to partition and retain experience. Past trajectories are grouped into clusters, each of which maintains state using one memory system from a fixed library. Before each run, an LLM-based router either reuses an existing cluster or creates a new one, basing its decision on the problem statement, memory system descriptions, and cluster summaries. We also introduce Context Memory Adapter, which mediates between the memory system and the agentic loop. We evaluate the architecture on a heterogeneous stream that interleaves 301 instances from the six CL-Bench tasks with 97 banking instances from $τ^3$-Bench. Across four language models and five matched rollouts, Routed Continual Learning raises normalized reward over a single global in-context learning (ICL) history by 15-30% of the maximum attainable normalized reward, achieves higher mean normalized reward than ICL-only clustering, and reduces estimated API cost by 65-72% relative to Global ICL. These results support approaching continual learning as a routing problem that chooses both a learning mechanism and a state cluster rather than committing to one representation globally. △ Less

We performed high-resolution $N$-body simulations of dwarf galaxies orbiting in a static Milky Way-like potential comprising a disc, bulge, and dark matter halo. Each dwarf comprised a Plummer stellar distribution embedded in a cuspy or cored generalised Navarro--Frenk--White dark matter halo. Idealised integrated surface brightness maps were constructed every 0.5 Gyr over 10 Gyr, and detectability was quantified through the projected area of stellar light brighter than limiting surface brightnesses, $μ_{\rm lim}=29$--$32$ mag arcsec$^{-2}$, chosen to span depths relevant to ARRAKIHS, LSST, and Euclid. The detectability of tidal features depends most strongly on progenitor stellar mass, orbital configuration, parameterised by the eccentricity proxy $e_0$, and evolutionary stage. Across this range, extended tidal debris is predominantly recovered for initially more radial configurations, mainly those with $e_0 \gtrsim 0.75$. In the simulated grid, detectable area is dominated by $M_\star=10^8$--$10^9 M_\odot$ progenitors, whereas $M_\star=10^6$--$10^7 M_\odot$ systems can undergo stripping without detectable debris. The detectable area evolves non-monotonically with time, reflecting the competition between stream formation, phase mixing, and the fading of diffuse debris below the adopted surface brightness limits. Cuspy and cored progenitors show similar evolution of the detectable area, with differences smaller than the scatter induced by orbital diversity. △ Less

From one library of 3x3 operators sharing a common two-clock latency, we construct three HDPUs (Canny edges, Harris corners, and a line tr… ▽ More Conventional vision pipelines route every pixel to a host processor, which must then wait for a complete frame before it can act and devote part of each frame period to routine pre-processing. We examine an alternative organisation in which this work is distributed across hardwired distributed processing units (HDPUs): small, fixed datapaths attached directly to the sensor, each committed to a single always-on task, operating truly in parallel on the same pixel stream and delivering compact results to the host. From one library of 3x3 operators sharing a common two-clock latency, we construct three HDPUs (Canny edges, Harris corners, and a line tracker that issues a steering decision) and run them side by side on a single camera stream in a low-cost Artix-7 FPGA, at a cost of 2,568 LUTs and an estimated 0.318 W. Every stage of every unit is bit-exact with its software model. In a full-system simulation with a datasheet camera model, each decision leaves its HDPU within 11.03 camera lines of the pixel that produced it, 22-67x sooner than the earliest moment at which a frame-based program on the host could respond; the HDPU latency varies by no more than 17 ns, whereas the host's own run times reached 12.5x their median. Offloading the three tasks returns 18-30% of a core to the host in every frame period at 640x480, and 63% of the frame period, with all threads in use, at 1080p. The cost of the units grows with frame width only through their line buffers, and in a closed-loop model of a line-following vehicle the HDPU raises the highest speed at which the line is held from 4.75 to 10.00 m/s. △ Less

    The Kinematically Hot, Extremely Metal-Poor C-19 Stellar Stream in DESI DR2

    Authors: Nasser Mohammed, Joseph Y. Tang, Ting S. Li, Sergey E. Koposov, Raymond G. Carlberg, Emma Jarvis, Andrew P. Li, Nathan Sandford, Gustavo E. Medina, Wenting Wang, Monica Valluri, Alexander H. Riley, Leandro Beraldo e Silva, Joan Najita, Mika Lambert, Songting Li, J. Aguilar, S. Ahlen, D. Bianchi, D. Brooks, T. Claybaugh, A. P. Cooper, A. de la Macorra, J. E. Forero-Romero, E. Gaztañaga , et al. (25 additional authors not shown)

    Abstract: Stellar streams are the result of a host galaxy's gravitational potential tidally disrupting satellite dwarf galaxies and globular clusters (GCs), causing them to grow leading and trailing tidal tails. The C-19 stellar… ▽ More Stellar streams are the result of a host galaxy's gravitational potential tidally disrupting satellite dwarf galaxies and globular clusters (GCs), causing them to grow leading and trailing tidal tails. The C-19 stellar stream is an extremely metal-poor stellar population, showing chemical abundance patterns characteristic of a globular cluster. However, its large velocity dispersion is difficult to reconcile with a conventional, purely baryonic, disrupting-GC progenitor. Current techniques for stream characterization are primarily applied to Gaia DR3, relying heavily on proper motion measurements. Using the Dark Energy Spectroscopic Instrument (DESI), which provides radial velocities and metallicites for over 10 million stars reaching significantly fainter magnitudes than comparable surveys, we employ a mixture model approach to jointly characterize stream populations in proper motions, radial velocities, and metallicities against a Milky Way halo background. By applying this framework to the C-19 stellar stream, we identify a total of 47 spectroscopically confirmed member stars, of which 41 are newly identified and only 6 were previously reported in the literature. In this work, we measure a velocity dispersion of $7.8^{+1.5}_{-1.3}$ km s$^{-1}$ and a mean metallicity of [Fe/H] = $-3.36^{+0.12}_{-0.10}$. We further identify a novel 'spur' feature within the stream. We conclude that our measurements are in line with previous works identifying C-19 as a 'hot', metal-poor stream. In forthcoming work, we will apply this approach to many more streams in the DESI footprint, enabling population-level comparisons with predictions from simulations. △ Less

    AtomTree: A Hierarchical Framework for State-Aware Embodied Instruction Following with LLMs

    Authors: Haotian Zhai, Tianming Sha, Junnan Li

    Abstract: Embodied instruction following in interactive environments requires agents to interpret natural language commands and complete long-horizon tasks. Existing planners either rely on large labeled trajectories, or use few-shot and zero-shot LLM prompting that produces a whole plan in a single serial reasoning process. The… ▽ More Embodied instruction following in interactive environments requires agents to interpret natural language commands and complete long-horizon tasks. Existing planners either rely on large labeled trajectories, or use few-shot and zero-shot LLM prompting that produces a whole plan in a single serial reasoning process. These approaches depend on linguistic common sense, commit to a plan from the initial instruction, and only update after failures, which weakens robustness and state awareness. We present AtomTree, a hierarchical framework for state-aware task planning with LLMs. AtomTree incrementally builds a tree of subgoals and actions, where the LLM decides at each step, from the current symbolic state, whether to further decompose or to output a grounded low-level action. A lightweight memory module summarizes past outcomes for reuse in later decisions. When a low-level action stalls, AtomTree triggers hierarchical backtracking replanning to return to the parent node and regenerate the subtree plan from the latest state, preventing continued drift along an incorrect trajectory. In zero-shot evaluation on the ALFRED benchmark, AtomTree outperforms prior LLM-based planners. Project page: https://zhaihaotian.github.io/atomtree-website/. △ Less

    Streaming Speech Recognition on a Microcontroller: a 65.7M-Parameter Transducer in 2 and 4 Bits on the ESP32-P4

    Authors: Billal Boumedine

    Abstract: We run Kroko, a 65.7M-parameter streaming Zipformer2 transducer for English, on the ESP32-P4, a microcontroller with two 400 MHz RISC-V cores, a 128-bit SIMD extension (PIE) and 32 MB of PSRAM, but no neural accelerator. Without any training, the encoder's 290 matrices are stored with 2 or 4 bits per weight in Hadamard-rotated groups: the width of each m… ▽ More We run Kroko, a 65.7M-parameter streaming Zipformer2 transducer for English, on the ESP32-P4, a microcontroller with two 400 MHz RISC-V cores, a 128-bit SIMD extension (PIE) and 32 MB of PSRAM, but no neural accelerator. Without any training, the encoder's 290 matrices are stored with 2 or 4 bits per weight in Hadamard-rotated groups: the width of each matrix comes from its measured sensitivity, and each matrix is first corrected toward the original model's output, then rounded with GPTQ and a search over the group norms. The released 71 MB of int8 weights become a 26.65 MB file. Activations are int16 between the matrix products and int8 into them. A C engine with hand-written PIE kernels processes each 1.28 s chunk of audio in 0.90 s on both cores, beam search included, and its PC build computes the same bits as the chip, so accuracy is measured on whole test sets. LibriSpeech WER is 4.34% on test-clean and 10.26% on test-other, against 3.94% and 9.17% for the released model; on FLEURS and Common Voice the gap grows to 2.3 and 1.1 points. A GPU simulator shows that the integer arithmetic costs nothing and that the whole gap comes from the weights, and an ablation on all of test-clean shows that GPTQ does most of the work. We also report the port's speed history, from 51.3 s to 0.62 s of encoder time per chunk, each design step measured again in one build, an attempt to reach 16 MB that stopped at 19.63 MB, and what did not work. We did not measure energy. The inference engine, the firmware and the 2/4-bit weights are available at https://github.com/BoumedineBillal/esp32-pie-asr. △ Less

    Kinetic linear stability theory for incompressible parallel shear flows with the full Boltzmann collision operator

    Authors: Vassilis Theofilis

    Abstract: …so that their results can be compared directly. Written in the peculiar velocity about a Chapman-Enskog base state, the collision operators are independent of the cross-stream coordinate and are formed once. Summation-by-parts Legendre collocation, weak diffuse-reflection wall conditions and a moment-consistent velocity derivative yield an energy-stable dis… ▽ More Modal and non-modal linear stability analysis of incompressible shear flows is performed for the first time by linearizing the Boltzmann equation and its full collision operator. In this kinetic linear stability theory (kLST) the hard-sphere operator and its BGK, ES-BGK and Shakhov relaxation models share the same discretization, so that their results can be compared directly. Written in the peculiar velocity about a Chapman-Enskog base state, the collision operators are independent of the cross-stream coordinate and are formed once. Summation-by-parts Legendre collocation, weak diffuse-reflection wall conditions and a moment-consistent velocity derivative yield an energy-stable discrete operator, verified to spectral accuracy by a manufactured solution. Numerical solution of the four-dimensional problem, comparable in size to a TriGlobal problem (Theofilis 2011), is made feasible by the fast spectral method of Gamba, Haack, Hauck and Hu (2017) for the full operator. Modal kLST uses matrix-free shift-invert Arnoldi iteration, preconditioned by an exact relaxation-model inverse. Non-modal kLST is based on an energy norm containing Chu's disturbance energy, a kinetic Reynolds-Orr equation identifying the mean shear as the only energy source. Transient growth and pseudospectra follow from the same preconditioned solves. For plane Couette and Poiseuille flows, the mixing layer and the Blasius boundary layer at Reynolds numbers 10^2-10^5, all four operators recover the Orr-Sommerfeld eigenvalues (Orszag 1971; Mack 1976) in the incompressible limit to a few parts in 10^7 for the models and 10^6 for the full operator. The optimal growth of Butler and Farrell (1992) and the resolvent norms of Trefethen et al. (1993) are reproduced to 0.1%, while the pseudospectra converge where ill-conditioned eigenvalues do not. △ Less

    Deterministic Streaming Lower Bounds for Approximate Maximum Clique and Maximum Independent Set

    Authors: Adithya Diddapur

    Abstract: We study the canonical \textsf{Maximum Clique} and \textsf{Maximum Independent Set} problems in the one-pass edge-arrival graph streaming setting. Here, the edges of some input graph $G = (V,E)$ are presented one at a time (possibly including deletions), before an algorithm needs to produce either a large clique or independent set at the end of the… ▽ More We study the canonical \textsf{Maximum Clique} and \textsf{Maximum Independent Set} problems in the one-pass edge-arrival graph streaming setting. Here, the edges of some input graph $G = (V,E)$ are presented one at a time (possibly including deletions), before an algorithm needs to produce either a large clique or independent set at the end of the stream, with the focus being on space complexity. We are interested in finding $β$-approximate solutions, for any $β\geq 1$. Previous work gave an algorithm using $\tilde{O}\left(n^2/β^2\right)$ bits of space, together with a corresponding $\tildeΩ\left(n^2/β^2\right)$ two-party communication lower bound [Halldórsson et al., ICALP'12], seeming to resolve the problem. However, their algorithm crucially relies on randomness, and the best known deterministic algorithm remains a folklore derandomisation using $O\left(n^2/β\right)$ bits of space, leaving a (deterministic) gap of size $\tilde{O}(β)$. We make progress towards this gap with a strong lower bound for a restricted range of $β$: whenever $β\leq \sqrt{n}/36\log n$, any deterministic algorithm for either problem must use $Ω\left(\frac{n^2}{β\cdot\log n}\right)$ bits of space. Our proof is via a two-party one-way communication lower bound, and highlights the power of randomness when approaching either of these problems. △ Less

    Geometry Matters in Packed 3D Attention

    Authors: Amil Khan, Anantajit Subrahmanya, B. S. Manjunath

    Abstract: Packing a 3D volume into a token sequence is not merely long-sequence modeling. Once the volume is packed, sequence order no longer encodes the geometry of the sample---so a positional mechanism indexed by sequence position can learn the memory layout rather than the object. This is a general failure mode for attention… ▽ More Packing a 3D volume into a token sequence is not merely long-sequence modeling. Once the volume is packed, sequence order no longer encodes the geometry of the sample---so a positional mechanism indexed by sequence position can learn the memory layout rather than the object. This is a general failure mode for attention over packed geometric data. We introduce PACK3D, a local-global attention operator that separates memory layout from attention geometry. PACK3D keeps local neighborhood attention exact, compresses only distant context into contiguous 3D block summaries, and indexes every query-key interaction with axial RoPE on true token-grid coordinates rather than packed positions. Because the rotation is applied before a block is summarized, the query-to-summary logit remains a weighted sum of query-relative 3D displacements; compressed global context therefore stays spatially interpretable after packing, instead of collapsing to anonymous pooled content. We measure the failure mode directly. On a controlled synthetic task---train on one packing, change only the memory order of the tokens, hold the physical labeling rule fixed---learned positional embeddings and packed-sequence RoPE solve the training layout perfectly yet collapse under Morton, block-contiguous, and random repacking, while axial grid-coordinate RoPE stays exact throughout. The same coordinate system carries to real data: on Allen WTC-11 hiPSC microscopy, PACK3D improves over a parameter-matched 3D U-Net on the 15-structure joint task and over a matched learned-position local-global baseline, with controls isolating the coordinate system, not encoder capacity, as the source of the gain. A fused Triton implementation matches windowed-attention latency at 65k tokens while supplying the long-range context windowing cannot---so the operator is not only correct but trainable at scale. △ Less

    On the Generalization Gap of Streaming Federated Learning with Markovian Data

    Authors: Masoud Kavian, Milad Sefidgaran, Youssef Attia El Hili, Malik Tiomoko, Abdellatif Zaidi

    Abstract: Streaming federated learning (SFL) allows clients to train a shared model from continuously arriving data without retaining large local datasets. Such… ▽ More Streaming federated learning (SFL) allows clients to train a shared model from continuously arriving data without retaining large local datasets. Such streams often exhibit temporal dependence, yet its effect on generalization remains poorly understood. In this paper we study the expected generalization gap of the aggregated model when client data follow Markov processes, using information-theoretic and rate-distortion techniques. Our framework accommodates heterogeneous Markovian dynamics across clients and distinguishes temporal dependence from information acquired during local learning. We establish bounds under cross-entropy loss, specializing them to Linear Next-Token Prediction (LNTP) and Self-Attention Next-Token Prediction (SANTP). These results quantify the effects of client count, communication rounds, sample size, data dimension, mixing time, and context length. In particular, they reveal a communication--generalization tradeoff: although frequent aggregation may help optimization, it can weaken generalization guarantees. They also suggest a potential advantage of distributed over centralized streaming learning under a fixed total data budget. Temporal dependence enters through the square root of the mixing time. By analyzing the history-state process, we obtain refined bounds with improved context-length dependence under suitable mixing conditions. Experiments on GPT-style language-model pretraining with TinyStories and real-world time-series forecasting illustrate how collaboration and communication frequency influence generalization under temporal dependence △ Less

    AskStream: When and What to Ask Proactively under Uncertainty in Streaming Video Understanding

    Authors: Lu Wang, Zhuoran Jin, Longxiang Wang, Junhong Chen, Yupu Hao, Yao Xu, Daojian Zeng, Yubo Chen, Kang Liu, Guang Liu, Jun Zhao

    Abstract: Recent advances in multimodal large language models have increased interest in interaction paradigms for video understanding. However, existing proactive paradigms typically assume… ▽ More Recent advances in multimodal large language models have increased interest in interaction paradigms for video understanding. The evolution from offline to streaming and proactive interaction has gradually shifted models from passive responders toward more proactive agents that can decide when to answer. However, existing proactive paradigms typically assume that the information required to answer a query is complete, leaving models to only wait or answer. In the real world, however, information is often incomplete due to uncertainty from either the video or the user. In proactive paradigms, such uncertainty can cause the model to wait forever or generate highly hallucinatory responses. We therefore argue that proactive interaction should be redefined with a third action: ask, allowing models to actively ask for missing information. To study when and what models should ask before answering, we introduce AskStream, the first multi-turn benchmark spanning video and user uncertainty for streaming video understanding, with four task categories and eight subtasks. It contains 438 videos with 2,714 manually annotated frame-level time windows across three types: wait, ask, and answer. Across both multiple-choice and open-ended settings, even the strongest model attains only 52.18% on our proposed Ask-F1 metric, highlighting substantial limitations in proactive information seeking under uncertainty. △ Less

This behaviour is observed across two segmentation backbones and remains consistent under variations in voxel resolution, geometric association, and temporal sampling density. Overall, conditions that reduce world-space recurrence can increase apparent aggregate stability, demonstrating that semantic consistency must be interpreted together with observation support. Our findings establish observation persistence as an essential conditioning variable for evaluating long-horizon semantic reliability and provide a framework for analysing temporal semantic behaviour beyond image-level prediction. △ Less

During recognition, template matching is performed using axonal delays between feature-detecting and template-detec… ▽ More Energy consumption is a major concern for modern artificial intelligence (AI) applications. The brain is thought to achieve energy-efficient computation through sparse coding schemes. By emulating the spiking dynamics of the brain, neuromorphic chips hold the potential to realize energy-efficient AI. In this work, we use birdsong to demonstrate that auditory object recognition can be achieved using ultra-sparse coding. Feature-detecting neurons tuned to short temporal features ($\sim$37 ms), sampled from exemplar syllables, transform continuous auditory signals into streams of ultra-sparse spikes that mark the onsets and offsets of features within the signal. Spike patterns elicited by the exemplars are stored as templates. During recognition, template matching is performed using axonal delays between feature-detecting and template-detecting neurons. Recognition is further enhanced by an additional group of specialized binary neurons trained to discriminate between syllable categories with subtle differences. When tested on Bengalese finch songs, our approach achieved error rates below 5% using 10 to 40 exemplars per syllable type. We then implemented this recognition algorithm using spiking neural networks on Intel's neuromorphic chip, Loihi, and found that response latencies remain constant (approximately 17 ms per syllable), regardless of the number of templates used. Our results demonstrate the potential of neuromorphic chips for low-latency, low-energy recognition of complex sounds, including speech. △ Less

    Proba: A High-Performance, Low-Traffic Probabilistic Spatial Memory Streaming Prefetcher

    Authors: Yinting Huang, Jacky Wong, Sam Ainsworth

    Abstract: Spatial Memory Streaming (SMS) prefetchers exploit recurring spatial access patterns across memory regions to predict future accesses. In this work, we present Proba, a feedback-d… ▽ More Spatial Memory Streaming (SMS) prefetchers exploit recurring spatial access patterns across memory regions to predict future accesses. Despite their effectiveness, existing SMS-based designs suffer from coarse control over aggressiveness and limited adaptability to dynamic program behavior and historical patterns. In this work, we present Proba, a feedback-directed SMS-based prefetcher that integrates probabilistic pattern updates, marginal accuracy decision-making, and pattern accumulation over several trigger features to achieve fine-grained accuracy-coverage trade-offs. When accuracy deteriorates, Proba moderates its aggressiveness to prioritize accurate prefetches; conversely, when accuracy rises, it expands its coverage. Proba achieves higher speedups than any other SMS-based prefetcher while being vastly more efficient in DRAM traffic. This culminates in a large performance gap between Proba and other techniques in multicore and DRAM-constrained scenarios without harming single-threaded throughput. △ Less

However, it remains unclear which post-training changes how much a model's truth judgement depends on the poster's social class, and how such a change happens inside the model. This paper investigates job-based doubt, i.e., judging a claim false more often when the poster has a lower-status job, using fact-checked claims with randomised poster profiles in base and post-trained checkpoints of six model families. Our results reveal that: 1) post-training creates such doubt only with the open Tülu-3 recipe (OLMo-2 32B, Tülu-3 70B), not with Meta's, Alibaba's or Mistral's; the effect is under one percentage point but statistically reliable, exceeding training drift and replicating on independent claim sets; 2) in OLMo-2 32B, the model does not start noticing the poster's job more, but starts interpreting it differently: a lower-status job pushes the base model toward "true" and the post-trained model toward "false"; and 3) this flip is carried by a single residual-stream direction and happens at supervised fine-tuning. The mechanism was confirmed on 400 new claims with pre-specified predictions. None of seven deployed commercial models doubts lower-status posters more. Our work underscores the need to audit speaker bias per training recipe. △ Less

    Accelerating Nonlinear MPC by Behavior Cloning with Separated Timescales: Application to Nuclear Reactor Load-Following

    Authors: Perceval Beja-Battais, Alain Grosset{ê}te, Nicolas Vayatis

    Abstract: …the physical knowledge of multiscale systems in which slow state variables, fast (stiff) state variables, and predictable exogenous inputs evolve at different timescales. We propose Structured Representation Learning for Behavior Cloning (SRLBC), an architecture designed to leverage the physical knowledge of multiscale systems in which slow state variables, fast (stiff) state variables, and predictable exogenous inputs evolve at different timescales. On 500 long-horizon roll-outs of a pressurized water reactor (PWR) load-following benchmark -a high-dimensional, stiff industrial system with challenging safety constraints -and across 20 training seeds, separated embeddings systematically outperform shared embedding baselines on cumulative cost, imitation accuracy and constraint satisfaction. The resulting standalone policies approach NMPC closed-loop performance at a computational cost 15 times lower on average. Since standalone policies still leave a few percent of trajectories infeasible, we embed the learned policies in a safety-augmented approximate NMPC framework, in which the optimizer is only called when the predicted input sequence is not certified as safe. This yields controllers still 6.8 times faster than the NMPC controller on average, with a feasibility (i.e., constraint satisfaction) of at least 99.8% for all architectures, SRLBC retaining the lowest closed-loop cost. Under distribution shift, standalone BC policies degrade sharply: SRLBC degrades the least, and once safety-augmented, it retains a feasibility above 98% with the lowest closed-loop cost. Beyond the quantitative results, Jacobian-based sensitivity analysis shows that the resulting controller relies on a sparse and less redundant set of latent features, each attributable to a single group of physical variables, and aligned with the physical timescale decomposition of the system. △ Less

    GPA-VGGT: Geometry-Preserving Acceleration of VGGT

    Authors: Muyeol Sung, Jangwon Kim, Jongwoo Lim

    Abstract: …pass, making them powerful backbones for multi-view 3D perception. These methods, however, come with a drop… ▽ More Feed-forward geometry models such as VGGT recover camera parameters, depth maps, and point maps from one to hundreds of views in a single forward pass, making them powerful backbones for multi-view 3D perception. However, VGGT's global attention is quadratic in the number of frames and dominates end-to-end runtime on long sequences, which has motivated a range of training-free efficiency methods such as token merging. These methods, however, come with a drop in accuracy that makes them hard to use in place of VGGT. We propose GPA-VGGT, a training-free acceleration method that replaces global attention in early blocks with frame attention augmented by a shared cross-frame K/V set. In the remaining blocks, we merge only keys and values while preserving all queries, restore the attention mass of the tokens each merged key represents, and randomly project the matching metric to 128 dimensions to reduce matching cost. Across diverse multi-view geometry tasks, GPA-VGGT provides training-free acceleration at speedups comparable to prior work while preserving the information required for precise geometric estimation, retaining over 99% of VGGT's pose accuracy under AUC@3. △ Less

The central design maintains visual and geometric histories on a common timeline. Noise-aligned overlapping continuation places generated history at the prescribed sampling noise level and commits only newly generated latents. The same generator supports estimated, native, and on-demand simulator depth, while cached and incremental execution paths produce bitwise-identical outputs. Appearance changes and geometric control branches operate through the same interface without modifying committed history. The measured steady-state DiT execution path achieves real-time generation throughput, demonstrating the efficiency of UniWorld for continuous visual world simulation. Without task-specific fine-tuning, this framework achieves competitive zero-shot performance on eight unseen real-world datasets and scales up to 700 distinct event types. △ Less

Compared with the base model, AnyGameRenderer better preserves entity correspondence: temporal match rises from 50.9% to 62.9%, missed count changes fall from 34.7% to 21.3%, and fewer entities are hallucinated or left unrendered.     Retrieve in Time, Correct in Frequency

    Authors: Yuze Fan, Yue Cao, Pengjie Gao, Haojia Gao, Guangqiu Guo, Ziyue Zhang, Junbo Tan, Bokui Chen, Zhuo Zou, Xueqian Wang

    Abstract: Frozen vision-language-action (VLA) policies generate temporally extended action chunks, but long-horizon manipulation remains vulnerable to accumulated execution error and visual aliasing across task stages. Successful rollouts provide useful corrective evidence, yet current frame retrieval can return progress-misaligned actions,while direct replay or time-domain fusion can overwrite the reactive structure of the policy proposal. We introduce Retrieve in Time, Correct in Frequency (RTCF), a training-free test-time correction framework that improves frozen VLA performance with low model-side overhead.RTCF separates which experience to retrieve from which part of its action to transfer. Progressive Memory Alignment (PMA) causally aligns the growing visual execution history with complete successful trajectories through incrementally updated monotonic frontiers, jointly identifying a relevant memory and the current aligned memory position without stage labels. From the aligned action chunk,RTCF transfers a coefficient-wise-clipped low-frequency residual on motion channels. Higher-frequency components and gripper decisions remain inherited from the frozen policy. Across four LIBERO suites and 2,000 episodes per condition, RTCF raises aggregate success from 86.4% to 88.4% and improves LIBERO-Long from 61.6% to 68.6%.These gains require no parameter updates, repeated VLA inference, or additional GPU resources: correction can be performed on the client CPU after a single policy invocation, and the median latencies sum to only 10.99 ms per action chunk △ Less

    Bet on Features: Anytime-Valid and Feature-Aware Auditing of Conditional Quantile Forecasters

    Authors: Ivane Antonov, Sohom Mukherjee, Richard Pibernik, Yo Joong Choe

    Abstract: …conditional quantile forecasts are widely used for sequential decisions under asymmetric costs. Further, existing backtests do not take into account that the notion of calibration is, in… ▽ More Black-box conditional quantile forecasts are widely used for sequential decisions under asymmetric costs. Once deployed, such forecasters must be monitored continuously as data streams drift and regimes change; this invalidates standard, fixed-horizon backtests for calibration. Further, existing backtests do not take into account that the notion of calibration is, in fact, information-dependent: forecasts can look calibrated to an auditor with coarse information while being miscalibrated to an auditor with richer information. We develop a distribution-free and game-theoretic testing framework for continuously auditing such forecasters with dependent, potentially nonstationary observations. We first formalize notions of conditional quantile calibration relative to the contextual features available to the auditor, showing how coarser information can limit detection. We then identify detectable alternative classes and, for contextual bets linear in the features, derive finite-time detection guarantees without an i.i.d. assumption. The resulting evidence processes provide interpretable, "feature-aware" evidence for miscalibration. We empirically validate these methods on simulated and real data, finding that a popular time series forecaster (Chronos-2) is highly miscalibrated w.r.t. multiple relevant features. △ Less

    Abstract: Context compaction lets long-horizon coding agents continue beyond their context windows, but can erase the state needed to finish a task. It covers 35 SWE-bench repairs, 146 agentic sessions, and 106 production chains. Across 14 summarizer backbones, stable damage ranges from 5-64% of 22 full-history successes: Claude Opus 4.8 preserves the most repairs, while DeepSeek-Flash damages the most. LLM summaries retain at most 16.0% of recurring details, compared with 79.1% for mechanical extraction; production summaries retain 15.7% at the first boundary. Failures recur across methods: one repair is damaged by 13/14 backbones, and another by all 5 prompts. These results show that reliable compaction requires preserving the progress needed to complete a task, and that retaining relevant information alone does not ensure successful continuation. On unseen ALFWorld tasks, SCOPE reaches 89.6% success, improving over GRPO by 23.2 percentage points with 46.7% fewer interactions. Scaling to Qwen3-32B, SCOPE achieves 99.2% success on seen ALFWorld tasks and 94.0% on unseen tasks. △ Less

    Authors: Senthil Palanisamy, Satpal Singh Rathore, Pratyush Kumar Patnaik, Shubhanshu Khatana, Gunjan Paul, Abhishek Anand

Calibrated stereo rigidly coupled to a synchronized inertial stream is not new; research-grade headsets deliver it. What does not exist is an open version: those platforms are closed products a third party cannot build or extend, and the consumer cameras used elsewhere are monocular and rolling-shutter. The instrument is the least reproducible part of an otherwise open pipeline. It pairs a hardware-synchronized global-shutter stereo camera with a 6-axis IMU, an embedded Linux SBC for on-device encoding, and a real-time microcontroller that bridges the camera and inertial clock domains to a residual of 700 microseconds and watchdogs the recording against silent failure. We release the full stack alongside the device: CAD, firmware, recording and time-synchronization tooling, and our corpus, roughly 550 hours of stereo video over 1,462 sessions and 25 contributors, every device shipping its own stereo calibration. The corpus ships with model-predicted annotation layers rather than raw video alone, including per-frame 3D hand reconstructions spanning essentially the whole timeline; these are automatic predictions, not human-verified ground truth, and we report them as such. We characterize the instrument along sensor fidelity, data utility, and six months of distributed field deployment. Our system is not the highest fidelity egocentric rig; it is the cheapest one a reader of this paper can reproduce, and the only corpus here that others can extend rather than only consume. △ Less

    FlatCake: A Flat KVCache Storage Architecture for LLM Serving

    Authors: Xin Huang, Teng Ma, Zhuoran Li, Yibo Zhao, Zhaoshi Li, Qianchao Zhu, Wei Yang, Chenxi Wang, Tao Xie, Youwei Zhuo

    Abstract: …reused at session scope, FlatCake places each block directly onto its target medium in session-contiguous, layer-major order at creation time and never migrates it. FlatCake streams coalesced SSD reads directly into a bounded GPU window via GPUDirect Storage and decouples DRAM and SSD request queues to prevent head-of-line blocking. On a production Claude Co… ▽ More KV cache reuse accelerates LLM serving, but existing systems manage host DRAM and NVMe SSD as a tiered hierarchy where DRAM acts as an intermediate cache. Production LLM workloads break both premises of this hierarchy. First, bimodal reuse defeats DRAM temporal filtering, since workloads are dominated either by one-shot blocks with long reuse distances or by multi-gigabyte agentic sessions that exceed DRAM capacity, putting SSD on the critical read path. Second, DRAM staging and eviction-ordered layout bottleneck SSD restoration. Holding host staging buffers for in-flight prefixes causes contention under load that aborts prefetches, while incremental eviction scatters a session's blocks across the SSD and prevents large sequential reads. We present FlatCake, a flat KV storage architecture that exposes host DRAM and NVMe SSD as peer media in a unified 64-bit address space. Exploiting the fact that KV blocks are write-once, immutable, and reused at session scope, FlatCake places each block directly onto its target medium in session-contiguous, layer-major order at creation time and never migrates it. FlatCake streams coalesced SSD reads directly into a bounded GPU window via GPUDirect Storage and decouples DRAM and SSD request queues to prevent head-of-line blocking. On a production Claude Code trace with DeepSeek-V4-Flash, FlatCake sustains a 94% cache hit rate as load increases (versus 64% for Mooncake), eliminates Mooncake's $6.5\times$--$6.7\times$ SSD read amplification, and reduces peak-load mean TTFT by $1.6\times$ while improving total token throughput by $1.6\times$. Across 8K--128K contexts on Llama-3.1-70B, FlatCake further reduces mean TTFT by up to $2.4\times$ over Mooncake and $1.8\times$ over Mooncake-GDS. △ Less

    Tracking the Discriminative Axis: Dual Prototypes for Test-Time OOD Detection Under Covariate Shift

    Authors: Wooseok Lee, Jin Mo Yang, Saewoong Bahk, Hyung-Sin Kim

    Abstract: For reliable deployment of deep-learning systems, out-of-distribution (OOD) detection is indispensable. In the real world, where test-time inputs often arrive as streaming mixtures of in-distribution (ID) and OOD samples under evolving covariate shifts, OOD samples are domain-constrained and bounded by the environment, and both ID and OOD are jointly affected by the same covariate factors. Existing methods typically assume a stationary ID distribution, but this assumption breaks down in such settings, leading to severe performance degradation. We empirically discover that, even under covariate shift, covariate-shifted ID (csID) and OOD (csOOD) samples remain separable along a discriminative axis in feature space. Building on this observation, we propose DART, a test-time, online OOD detection method that dynamically tracks dual prototypes -- one for ID and the other for OOD -- to recover the drifting discriminative axis, augmented with multi-layer fusion and flip correction for robustness. Extensive experiments on a wide range of challenging benchmarks, where all datasets are subjected to 15 common corruption types at severity level 5, demonstrate that our method significantly improves performance, yielding 15.32 percentage points (pp) AUROC gain and 49.15 pp FPR@95TPR reduction on ImageNet-C vs. Textures-C compared to established baselines. These results highlight the potential of the test-time discriminative axis tracking for dependable OOD detection in dynamically changing environments. △ Less
