    Compositional Diffusion Planning with Chunk Selection and Reachability Measure

    Authors: Chanyoung Park, Youngjoong Kim, Jaesik Park

    Abstract: Compositional diffusion planners have demonstrated remarkable success on long-horizon diffusion planning by sampling short trajectories, namely chunks, and composing them into a long trajectory. However, averaging overlapping chunks can induce the mode-averaging problem, resulting in discontinuous trajectories. Several… ▽ More Compositional diffusion planners have demonstrated remarkable success on long-horizon diffusion planning by sampling short trajectories, namely chunks, and composing them into a long trajectory. However, averaging overlapping chunks can induce the mode-averaging problem, resulting in discontinuous trajectories. Several works aim to address this problem, whereas a non-negligible portion of plans remains infeasible. In this work, we propose Selective Compositional Diffuser (SCD), handling this problem by measuring reachability. To this end, we present a self-supervised learning method for the reachability classifier. This is trained on offline data and generated chunks jointly to address potential distributional mismatch, while reducing the bias inherited from the generated data. Based on this, we construct a graph whose edge costs represent whether consecutive chunks are reachable, and traverse the graph to find effective yet efficient routes. Lastly, we generate bridging trajectories and refine the composed trajectory for smooth transitions. We evaluate our method on OGBench stitching tasks. It practically reduces incompatible mode compositions and the mode-averaging problem compared to the studied baselines. Across tasks, SCD improves success rates, reaching state-of-the-art results on HumanoidMaze-Large and Giant tasks. △ Less

    The Role of Quantization in Loss Spikes

    Authors: Zaiqian Chen, Aditya Somasundaram, Sophie Li, Sean McLeish, John Kirchenbauer, Tom Goldstein, Micah Goldblum

    Abstract: …behind their effectiveness. Together, our results identify finite precision error as a hidden driver of time-bomb spikes and show that suppressing this error is key to stable long-horizon pretraining. ▽ More Loss spikes in language model pretraining can waste substantial compute and derail otherwise healthy training runs. A primary driver of these instabilities is the growth and divergence of output logits in the final language modeling (LM) head. In this work, we study a particularly costly form of this instability associated with output logit growth: delayed, terminal divergences that emerge only after prolonged training, which we call time-bomb spikes. We find that these instabilities are preceded by rapidly growing quantization error in the LM head, which eventually culminates in a loss spike. Crucially, reducing numerical precision accelerates time-bomb spikes, while increasing precision improves stability. We then propose a simple yet effective mitigation that replaces AdamW on the LM head with SGD with momentum, suppressing logit growth and improving stability over long training horizons. Finally, we find that existing mitigation techniques from the literature (along with our own) all reduce quantization error, suggesting a shared numerical mechanism behind their effectiveness. Together, our results identify finite precision error as a hidden driver of time-bomb spikes and show that suppressing this error is key to stable long-horizon pretraining. △ Less

Code is available at https://github.com/cruiseresearchgroup/SGNO. ▽ More Neural operators efficiently simulate dynamical systems by repeatedly predicting the next state. However, small errors at each step can accumulate, causing long-term predictions to drift from the true trajectory. Long-term error reflects both the propagation of earlier prediction errors and new errors introduced at each step. How to reduce these errors is therefore a critical question. We propose the Spectral Generator Neural Operator (SGNO), which combines a linear estimate of the next state with a learned correction. The linear component uses consecutive states in the training data to fit a map from the current state to the next. A neural network corrects this prediction by learning changes that the linear model does not capture, including nonlinear interactions. Both components are trained jointly toward the same next-state target. Experiments across 13 periodic PDE configurations in one to three dimensions show that SGNO accurately learns state evolution on linear tasks and reduces 100-step rollout error by 13-74% over the strongest baseline under the main comparison protocol on each nonlinear task. Longer-horizon extrapolation tests show lower late-window prediction errors on several nonlinear tasks. These results show that combining fitted propagation with learned state-dependent correction can improve long-horizon autoregressive prediction on the evaluated PDE tasks. Code is available at https://github.com/cruiseresearchgroup/SGNO. △ Less

    ATI-OPD: Adaptive Teacher Intervention for Stable Long-Sequence On-Policy Distillation

    Authors: Jiannan Guan, Youxin Jiang, Yutai Hou, Qiguang Chen, Yirong Zeng, Haitao Wang, Dengyun Peng, Zheng Yan, Jinhao Liu, Tao Ye, Dandan Tu, Wanxiang Che

    Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student on its own responses using token-level feedback from a teacher. As responses grow longer, the student's prefix can depart from the teacher's own reasoning, weakening its feedback on later tokens. Updates based on this feedback can reinforce the same deviations in later responses and destabilize training. Prior approaches such as length control and loss… ▽ More On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student on its own responses using token-level feedback from a teacher. As responses grow longer, the student's prefix can depart from the teacher's own reasoning, weakening its feedback on later tokens. Updates based on this feedback can reinforce the same deviations in later responses and destabilize training. Prior approaches such as length control and loss reweighting limit learning from problematic responses but leave their prefixes unchanged. Fixed cutoffs can also discard later reasoning. We propose \textbf{Adaptive Teacher Intervention for On-Policy Distillation (ATI-OPD)} to improve subsequent teacher guidance by revising the student's reasoning prefix. ATI-OPD replaces the earliest high-disagreement segment with a short teacher segment, then lets the student resume its own reasoning from the revised text. To handle renewed disagreement, it excludes persistently divergent suffixes from the distillation loss while retaining useful student continuation. Training diagnostics show less teacher-student disagreement, fewer gradient spikes, and fewer extremely long responses. Across five mathematical reasoning benchmarks and two teacher-student pairs, ATI-OPD improves average student-only accuracy over standard OPD. △ Less

    Authors: Liang Yuan, Xiaohu Du, Ruikun Luo, Jinze Wang, Mashud Rana, Kaibin Wang, Jiangwen Zhou, Bo Li, Xiaoyu Xia

    Abstract: …decoding with private drafts did not include the secret code in its answers, yet sent the code to the cloud in rejected drafts. In a planted-secret audit, speculative decoding with private drafts did not include the secret code in its answers, yet sent the code to the cloud in rejected drafts. We present ADLER, a decoding protocol for streaming LLM APIs in which every message to the cloud depends only on the public view and the answer the user sees, regardless of how the client combines the two models or how the cloud behaves. A client-side leakage ledger then bounds how much the answer itself reveals relative to a public reference process built from the cloud's replies. In the audit, no ADLER configuration exposed the secret beyond the answer. On GSM8K math problems with numerical values masked in the cloud's input, ADLER with an unlimited leakage budget raises the exact-match accuracy of a 0.5B client from 0.41 to 0.83 and of a 1.5B client from 0.67 to 0.85 under lenient answer parsing, close to the 0.92 achieved by uploading the full problem. It also exceeds a zero-leakage program baseline (0.69) by 0.14 and 0.16, respectively. In this baseline, the cloud writes a program that the client runs offline using the private numbers. The ledger records the disclosure charge associated with this gain; budgets of 16 nats or less leave accuracy near the cloud-only baseline on GSM8K. △ Less

    Abstract: …it names a changed field and computes its executed value. Yet in interactive worlds, this rewards copying: a model that merely preserves persistent state appears accurate without predicting any consequence of an action. We introduce ScratchWorld, an execution-grounded diagnostic benchmark that treats Scratch projects as executable worlds and derives every label from replay on a pinned Scratch VM, including next-state transitions, hidden variables, causal traces, and counterfactual outcomes. Its primary metric, value-aware changed-field F1 ($F_1^{\mathrm{VA}}$), credits a prediction only when it names a changed field and computes its executed value. Model-free anchors make the confound explicit: a no-change prior attains 91.4% full-state field accuracy on synthetic long-horizon items and 0% changed-field credit on non-empty one-step items, whereas VM replay attains 100% wherever the corresponding metric is defined. Across seven frontier LLMs prompted as world models, state-only $F_1^{\mathrm{VA}}$ reaches at most 30.0% on controlled synthetic mechanisms and declines steeply as projects approach real-world complexity. Revealing the full program raises every model's value-aware score by 22.3-51.7 points and substantially reorders the models, yet the best reaches only 55.4%, an execution gap that persists even with the rules visible. Auxiliary diagnostics complete the error profile: causal-event accuracy remains within 2.5 points of the 50% no-event baseline, with no correct exact chain; counterfactual prediction trades changed-outcome accuracy against invariance; hidden-state accuracy degrades roughly twice as fast as full-state overlap over long horizons. In a word, ScratchWorld separates what a model knows about the rules from what it can actually execute. △ Less

    Abstract: …ability to pay. We present CADENCE, Calendar-Aware Distributional Event Network for Cash-Flow Estimation, a compact causal Transformer that treats each transaction as a token and predicts the amount and a full, nonparametric timing distribution of every cash flow's next payment. Inheriting transaction semantics from an external labeler and a frozen text encoder keeps CADENCE compact yet general across the long tail of small-business counterparties, while predicting a calibrated distribution rather than an opaque embedding makes its outputs interpretable enough to feed existing underwriting models and power credit decisions. Ablations confirm the role of each design choice, with counterparty semantics driving recall on short histories, calendar inputs driving timing accuracy, and no-event supervision driving precision. Against the production FFT baseline, CADENCE improves one-day timing accuracy by 93.3% and next-event F1 by 6.7%, while producing calibrated distributions that serve directly as a confidence gate for downstream decisions. In an end-to-end credit evaluation on production applications, replacing FFT recurrence features with CADENCE features raises KS by 4.8%, raises the approval rate at a fixed loss rate by 18.2%, and lowers the loss rate at a fixed approval rate by 13.1%. △ Less

    Neural Stream Function Method for Electrical Impedance Tomography with Lipschitz Conductivities

    Authors: Xiaosheng Li

    Abstract: …but training physics informed neural networks for EIT and relating their training loss to reconstruction accuracy remain challenging. The method builds the conductivity constraints and prescribed boundary voltages into the neural trial functions and re… ▽ More Electrical impedance tomography (EIT) reconstructs the conductivity inside an object from voltage and current measurements on its boundary. The resulting inverse problem is nonlinear and severely ill-posed. Neural networks offer a flexible computational approach, but training physics informed neural networks for EIT and relating their training loss to reconstruction accuracy remain challenging. We develop a novel constrained neural stream function method for planar EIT with Lipschitz conductivities. The method builds the conductivity constraints and prescribed boundary voltages into the neural trial functions and replaces the second order conductivity equation with a first order system in strong form. The measured current is also used to construct a fixed baseline for the stream function. For this formulation, we derive error estimates that relate the sampled neural objective to errors in the physical boundary currents and the reconstructed conductivity. The error estimates separate the contributions from neural approximation, measurement noise, and sampling. Numerical experiments further demonstrate that the proposed method achieves high reconstruction accuracy with both exact and noisy boundary data. △ Less

A five-variant ablation suggests this gain is primarily attributable to the CTDE architecture when holding cooperative reward terms fixed, though not formally isolated with a single seed. By contrast, cooperative reward shaping and experience sharing show no clear descriptive benefit and may slightly reduce performance. These findings support the view that, for multi-station charging assignment, coordination should be embedded in the learning architecture rather than added only through heuristic cooperation terms. △ Less

    Authors: Giovanni Minelli, Giulio Turrisi, Lorenzo Amatucci, Victor Barasuol, Claudio Semini

    Abstract: …of robots that learn manipulation skills from interaction alone, without pretrained priors or demonstration data. This patient-specific normalisation substantially improves separation, yielding a candidate biomarker of tumour progression. Applied to parameterized 2D/3D confined cylinder flows and an unevenly heated cavity over Reynolds/Rayleigh-number ranges, the models generalize to unseen conditions and accurately predict sparse probe locations and integrated quantities. A dynamics-informed adaptive sampling strategy guided by frequency-spectrum variations reduces maximum errors by an order of magnitude versus uniform sampling at negligible cost. Coarse-mesh training also makes noise-augmented multi-step unrolling practical, improving long-horizon autoregressive predictions. The approach outperforms parametric DMD, sparse MLPs, U-Nets, and Fourier neural operators at lower cost, and enables system identification and state estimation from sparse observations. These results show that coarse-mesh representations, adaptive sampling, and noise-augmented unrolled training form an efficient framework for reduced-order modeling of parameterized unsteady flows without solver-resolution reconstruction. △ Less

Auxiliary-channel comparisons recover 80% of the snax loud-feature population per time bin. We introduce SWE-Bench ProMax, an expert-curated, multilingual code refactoring benchmark of 170 instances drawn from real commits across seven programming languages (Python, Java, TypeScript, Go, C, C++, and Rust).     Seq@K: Evaluating Language Models Under Sequential Feedback

    Authors: Thomson Yen, Gilbert Yang, Naimeng Ye, Minghui Chen, Dong Xia, Tianyi Shen, Yechan Jeon, Yaqin Chen, Daksh Mittal, Fangyu Wu, Hongseok Namkoong

    Abstract: …that measures whether a model can solve a problem within K sequential attempts, provided feedback after each previous failure. Unlike multi-turn agentic benchmarks that examine long-horizon planning, Seq@K isolates and formalizes the core capability of refinement from feedback. We evaluate Seq@K on a suite of 7 benchma… ▽ More Language-model agents differ fundamentally from prior intelligent systems in their ability to learn from natural language in context. This capability enables agents to iteratively refine their answers in response to feedback, a prevalent mode of operation in modern agentic workflows. However, the community primarily ranks models by metrics such as Pass@1 or Pass@K, which cannot capture how they utilize feedback on prior attempts. We introduce Seq@K, a natural evaluation protocol that measures whether a model can solve a problem within K sequential attempts, provided feedback after each previous failure. Unlike multi-turn agentic benchmarks that examine long-horizon planning, Seq@K isolates and formalizes the core capability of refinement from feedback. We evaluate Seq@K on a suite of 7 benchmarks and 16 frontier models, and show that Seq@K reveals a materially different model-selection landscape: under Seq@K, (1) model rankings often differ, (2) the Pareto frontiers admit more efficient configurations and (3) the gains from choosing the optimal model configuration can widen or shrink relative to those of Pass@K. We further show that analyzing model trajectories under Seq@K helps characterize iterative refinement behavior, such as the rate at which models address issues raised in feedback, or how rigidly they hold onto their own answers. Finally, we study how the nature of feedback affects models' iterative refinement, showing that practitioners must carefully design and benchmark the feedback loops of iterative agentic systems. Together, these results establish how Seq@K offers a more faithful metric for intelligent systems that typically operate in such feedback loop, and the necessity to consider Seq@K in evaluating models for these systems. △ Less

    cs.LG cs.CV

    Learning Without Clocks: Partition-Invariant Neural Network

    Authors: Han Wang, Jingyuan Bao, Juntao Wu, Saiao Zhou, Yuman Nie

    Abstract: Event cameras, spiking cochleas and similar sensors emit asynchronous events, yet most learning systems first bin them into frames or packets, so the output depends on how the stream was sliced. Networks whose state is updated exactly at every event and evolves in closed form between events are invariant to this slicing. We ask what more makes the output (i)… ▽ More Event cameras, spiking cochleas and similar sensors emit asynchronous events, yet most learning systems first bin them into frames or packets, so the output depends on how the stream was sliced. Networks whose state is updated exactly at every event and evolves in closed form between events are invariant to this slicing. We ask what more makes the output (i) independent of the processing order of simultaneous events and (ii) continuous in the time stamps, even when two events cross in time, while (iii) staying sensitive to temporal order. For arbitrary nonlinear event models we prove that (i) and (ii) are each equivalent to pairwise commutation of the event updates, and that once updates commute, all order dependence comes from their non-commutation with the inter-event dynamics and vanishes linearly in the time lag. This identifies a class of time-mediated non-commutative (TM) models and yields a resolution-robustness bound for any model. We construct the minimal TM layer, a learnable, provably Lipschitz generalization of the Hassenstein-Reichardt correlator; prove that no single additive layer can uniformly represent ordered pair interactions, whereas a three-dimensional TM state can; show that each TM layer adds two levels of temporal nesting versus one for additive layers; and give a single-layer higher-order construction (TM-k) and a stacking rule preserving all three properties. We instantiate these results as the Clock-Free Event (CFE) layer, linear in the number of events, give a topology-free variant for temporal point processes with closed-form likelihoods, and specify a preregistered clock-robustness protocol with a physical-time threat model for spike-retiming attacks. In preliminary development-set experiments, the TM state beats an otherwise identical additive state by 3.5-9.1 points on DVS128 Gesture and by 24.5 and 30.6 points on SHD and Speech Commands. △ Less

    Universal Short-Time Fourier Transform: Data-Adaptive Window-Length Selection for Time-Frequency Analysis

    Authors: Manan Mittal, Kanad Sarkar, Ryan M. Corey, John R. Buck, Andrew C. Singer

    Abstract: …expectation across the candidate bank yields a single spectrum equivalent to an STFT evaluated with a data-adaptive effective window, while an envelope-normalized synthesis stream guarantees exact overlap-add reconstruction. Evaluations on synthetic and marine bioacoustic signals demonstrate adaptive resolution tracking, enhanced spectral concentration, and… ▽ More The short-time Fourier transform (STFT) characterizes the local frequency content of time-varying signals by evaluating the discrete Fourier transform over a sliding analysis window. The STFT is governed by a fundamental resolution trade-off: a short window localizes rapid transients in time at the cost of frequency resolution, whereas a long window resolves closely spaced harmonics at the expense of temporal resolution. The Universal Short-Time Fourier Transform (U-STFT) resolves this trade-off sequentially by modeling the analysis window duration as a data-adaptive state variable that indexes the number of hops included in the current observation interval. At each frame, a bank of candidate window durations is scored using normalized order-three Rényi entropy to quantify joint time-frequency concentration. A window length trellis aggregates the resulting $\mathcal{O}(3^M)$ candidate window schedules with linear complexity per frame, guaranteeing a cumulative mix-loss regret bound of $\mathcal{O}((k+1)\log M)$ relative to any hindsight window schedule with $k$ resolution changes over $M$ frames. By linearity of the Fourier transform, posterior expectation across the candidate bank yields a single spectrum equivalent to an STFT evaluated with a data-adaptive effective window, while an envelope-normalized synthesis stream guarantees exact overlap-add reconstruction. Evaluations on synthetic and marine bioacoustic signals demonstrate adaptive resolution tracking, enhanced spectral concentration, and exact time-domain invertibility. △ Less

    When Should We Intervene at Test Time?

    Authors: Jiahao Zhang, Xinling Wen, Liangliang Li, Yu Chen

    Abstract: …loses accuracy overall into a useful choice on selected batches. It prevents severe failures caused by changes in arrival order and preserves gains across complete corruption streams. When a fixed rule already works well, PORTAL uses little feedback to retain its performance. Periodic label checks reveal when past evidence needs refreshing and help the contr… ▽ More Test-time adaptation helps a deployed model respond to changing inputs, but the same intervention can rescue one batch and damage the next. Updating normalization statistics, for example, can improve corrupted images yet fail when a batch contains mostly one class. Average accuracy hides these failures and cannot tell a model when to use an intervention. Target labels can guide this decision, but they are scarce and some choices are already clear. We introduce PORTAL, a controller that chooses when to deploy an intervention, which inputs to label, and when to stop querying. Each available prediction rule is a candidate, and its predictions are fixed before current feedback arrives. Every acquired label therefore evaluates all candidates on the same input. PORTAL combines these shared comparisons and directs queries toward candidates that can still overtake the current leader. It stops when the remaining labels cannot change the choice or when the possible accuracy gain no longer justifies another query. Our analysis links the required feedback to accuracy gaps and shared mistakes, explaining why some choices need much less evidence. Experiments show that this control turns an intervention that loses accuracy overall into a useful choice on selected batches. It prevents severe failures caused by changes in arrival order and preserves gains across complete corruption streams. When a fixed rule already works well, PORTAL uses little feedback to retain its performance. Periodic label checks reveal when past evidence needs refreshing and help the controller recover as conditions change. The acquired labels also train stronger candidates for future batches, and selection brings nearly all of their accuracy gains into deployment. The same feedback therefore improves both the choice made now and the predictions available to later batches. △ Less

    Authors: Ian Chuang, Hiya Shah, Vamsi Chilamkur, Iman Soltani, Claire Tomlin

With careful system identification, the policies transfer zero-shot to the real hand, using either motion capture or a single wrist-mounted depth camera, and prove remarkably capable and robust. On top of the task policy, we add a learned residual safety filter with adjustable conservativeness, letting us tune the balance between performance and safety and greatly reduce object drops. In 13.3 hours of real-robot evaluation over 4,800 unseen goals across 4 objects and diverse wrist orientations, the state-based policy reaches 95.2% of goals within 2 cm and 0.4 rad, and the filter cuts its drops by 75% while still reaching 91.5% of goals. The policies can also spin a lightbulb held in an arbitrary pose, reach out-of-distribution goals, withstand fast arm motion and perturbations, and, needing only a wrist camera, serve as portable skills such as screwing in a lightbulb. We release the full recipe as a platform for studying performance, reliability, and safety in high-dimensional dexterous systems. Videos and more are available at https://ian-chuang.github.io/dexsafe/. △ Less

    Multi-State Linear Attention with Generalized Second-Order Routing

    Authors: Hee Seung Hwang, Sanghyuk Chun, Xindi Wu, Olga Russakovsky, Zhiwei Deng

    Abstract: Self-attention plays a key role in long-sequence modeling, but its quadratic time complexity fundamentally limits the scaling of Large Language Models. Recently, Multi-State Linear Attention (MSLA) mechanisms have been proposed to make sequence modeling more efficient by splitting the context into segments and compress… ▽ More Self-attention plays a key role in long-sequence modeling, but its quadratic time complexity fundamentally limits the scaling of Large Language Models. Recently, Multi-State Linear Attention (MSLA) mechanisms have been proposed to make sequence modeling more efficient by splitting the context into segments and compressing each in its own memory state. MSLA aggregates the information extracted from each memory state using only the mean alignment between a query and each segment's keys, ignoring how the alignment is distributed within the segment. In order to model the relevance of a segment to a query more comprehensively and incorporate the variance of the alignment in the aggregation, we propose MERLIN (MomEnt Routing for multi-state LINear attention), a method that tracks the first and second moments of each segment's keys. Using the moments of keys, a query can recover both the mean and the variance of its alignment with each segment to perform a more content-aware aggregation of per-state readouts. Experiments on multi-state variants of Gated DeltaNet-1.3B and Mamba-2-780M show that MERLIN consistently outperforms existing aggregation methods on various long-context tasks. In particular, MERLIN outperforms baselines on RULER needle-in-a-haystack and long-context QA (+8.3% for Gated DeltaNet-1.3B and +7.0% for Mamba-2-780M) and diverse subtasks in LongBench (+13.5% and +12.0%). It even outperforms baselines that consume 4x more memory. MERLIN provides a simple and general routing mechanism for improving long-context capabilities of MSLA models. △ Less

    Abstract: …transfer of deposition, filtering and field gathering within the model rate-tolerance boundary. The controlled two-stream scan varies the beam drift while holding the initial spatial density, thermal samples, wavevector an… ▽ More Charge deposition in the particle-in-cell (PIC) method can be viewed as kernel density estimation on a spatial mesh. This allows the smoothing bandwidth to be selected through statistical criteria such as mean integrated squared error or cross-validation, but it also changes the electric field acting on the particles. Therefore, a selected bandwidth can alter the growth or damping of physical modes. We develop a dynamics-aware bandwidth-selection procedure that combines a density-error objective with constraints on specified linear modes. These constraints follow from the plasma equilibrium and the combined transfer of deposition, filtering and field gathering within the model rate-tolerance boundary. We demonstrate the need for equilibrium information through a controlled two-stream experiment. The controlled two-stream scan varies the beam drift while holding the initial spatial density, thermal samples, wavevector and fixed-filter transfer unchanged. Across four resolved equilibria approaching marginal stability, this filter reduces the measured growth rate by $0.7\%$ to $9.1\%$. Equilibrium-dependent interior settings satisfy the prescribed $10\%$ rate tolerance. We then apply the constrained selection rule to 85 Gaussian candidates, that reduces density and grid-field mean-square errors by $7.40\%$ and $3.70\%$ on independently evaluated snapshots. A separate projected-linear calculation at that exact width gives a ratio of mean individual growth rates of $0.9556$, with paired $95\%$ interval $[0.9453,0.9640]$, consistent with the prediction $0.9513$. Analysis of finite-marker loading and ensemble averaging explains how the measured rates relate to the response model. Together, these results demonstrate a filter-design procedure that combines statistical error reduction with subsequent validation of the selected linear dynamics. △ Less

    Rendering-Free Event Frame Simulation for Fast Reinforcement Learning of Autonomous Robot Navigation

    Authors: Dequan Ou, Austin Lopez, Quentin Missinne, Christophe De Wagter, Guido C. H. E. de Croon

    Abstract: …learning prohibitively slow. We propose an analytic event simulator that exploits what event cameras fundamentally measure - moving contrast edges - to synthesize event streams directly from scene geometry and ego-motion without visual rendering. Our simulator generates a 64x64 event observation in 17 microseconds on a single GPU - over 400x faster than a st… ▽ More Event cameras offer low latency, high dynamic range, and milliwatt-level power consumption, making them ideal for agile robots such as tiny drones navigating through cluttered environments. However, existing simulators generally render photorealistic images before converting them into events, making large-scale reinforcement learning prohibitively slow. We propose an analytic event simulator that exploits what event cameras fundamentally measure - moving contrast edges - to synthesize event streams directly from scene geometry and ego-motion without visual rendering. Our simulator generates a 64x64 event observation in 17 microseconds on a single GPU - over 400x faster than a state-of-the-art rendering pipeline - enabling end-to-end reinforcement learning for goal-directed obstacle avoidance to converge in under 30 minutes - three orders of magnitude faster than training on rendered events. Policies transfer zero-shot to physically based event simulations (ray-traced Isaac Sim). Our policy reaches 83% success in cluttered courses unseen during training (mid-difficulty, traversability probability approx. 0.57), at 2.4 m/s average goal velocity, and retains 70% in a simulated texture-dense indoor hall. Furthermore, we quantized the policy to a 1.2 MB int8 network running fully onboard a 56-gram Crazyflie equipped with a Prophesee GenX320 event camera and an OpenMV N6 microcontroller board. Without any fine-tuning, external processing, or positioning, the policy achieves an average success rate of 82% (5/5, 5/6, 4/6 flights) across three real-world obstacle courses of increasing difficulty. Code: https://github.com/tudelft/rendering-free-event-nav △ Less

Despite their growing importance, the security of MLTSF models against backdoor attacks remains entirely unexplored. To bridge this gap, we propose BadTime, the first effective backdoor attack tailored for MLTSF. BadTime can manipulate hundreds of future predictions toward a target pattern by injecting a subtle trigger. BadTime addresses two key challenges that become particularly severe in MLTSF: (i) the rapid dilution of local triggers over long horizons, and (ii) the extreme sparsity of backdoor signals under stealth constraints. To counter dilution, BadTime leverages inter-variable correlations, temporal lags, and data-driven initialization to design a distributed, lag-aware trigger whose influence persists over long-range forecasts. To overcome sparsity, it mixes label-similar and label-dissimilar poisoned samples and adopts a decoupled backdoor training objective that explicitly up-weights the sparse backdoor signal, enabling reliable learning at a poisoning rate as low as 1%. Extensive experiments show that BadTime significantly outperforms the state-of-the-art backdoor attack on time series forecasting by extending the attackable horizon from at most 12 timesteps to 720 timesteps (a 60x improvement) and reducing the MAE on target variables by 57% on average, while changing the average clean forecasting error by less than 1%. △ Less

However, all-to-all pairwise token comparison enforces a quadratic $\mathcal{O}(N^2)$ compute and memory barrier alongside severe softmax attention dilution as sequence length $N$ scales beyond the training horizon. Inspired by recent advances in algebraic graph learning (\textbf{WPNet}), this work introduces \textbf{GTFormer}, a syntax-free hierarchical Graph Neural Network (GNN) that replaces flat self-attention for general long-context sequence modeling. GTFormer organizes raw token streams into a bounded-degree hierarchy combining a balanced binary reduction tree ($\lceil \log_2 N \rceil$ depth), horizontal boundary-bridging shift edges, random expander shortcuts, multi-channel convex highway gating, and a top-down broadcast pass. Across eight long-context code, log, and multi-hop retrieval benchmarks trained exclusively on sequences of length $N \le 512$, GTFormer extrapolates zero-shot to $N = 4{,}194{,}304$ ($8192\times$) on a single commodity GPU in linear $\mathcal{O}(N)$ memory, maintaining near-perfect accuracy where standard and RoPE Transformers suffer from out-of-distribution attention collapse and out-of-memory exhaustion. △ Less

We study real-time audio-to-audio accompaniment: as a model hears an input audio stream (e.g., a singer singing), it has to also simultaneously generate in real-time a coherent accompanying stream (e.g., a guitar accompaniment). In this work, we propose a model design considering inevitable system delays in practical deployment with two design variables: future visibility $t_f$, the offset between the output playback time and the latest input time used for conditioning, and output chunk duration $k$, the number of frames emitted per call. We train Transformer decoders across a grid of $(t_f,k)$ and show two consistent trade-offs: increasing effective $t_f$ improves coherence by reducing the recency gap, but requires faster inference to stay within the latency budget; increasing $k$ improves throughput but results in degraded accompaniment due to a reduced update rate. Finally, we observe that naive maximum-likelihood streaming training is insufficient for coherent accompaniment where future context is not available, motivating advanced anticipatory and agentic objectives for live jamming. △ Less

    Strategyproof Deep Reinforcement Learning for Online Resource Allocation

    Authors: Fariba Dehghan, Sebastian Stein, Vahid Yazdanpanah, Stephanie Gauthier

    Abstract: …ensure strategyproofness and individual rationality, while the neural rule provides flexibility and imitation improves training efficiency. Empirically, on a four-resource stream with two partner pairs that a linear difference cannot represent, the neural rule achieves 97.8% of an offline welfare upper bound, compared with 93.8% for a linear learner and 89.0… ▽ More Online resource allocation faces two coupled challenges: (1) allocating scarce resources efficiently under uncertain future demand, and (2) ensuring that agents report task valuations and requirements truthfully (strategyproofness). Reinforcement Learning (RL) adapts allocation decisions through experience but does not necessarily ensure strategyproofness. Existing strategyproof RL uses a linear allocation rule, which limits the resource interactions it can represent. Neural mechanism design methods offer greater expressiveness, but provide only approximate incentive guarantees. Against this background, we introduce the first strategyproof deep RL mechanism for online resource allocation. It integrates three novel components: (i) a monotonic neural network that learns nonlinear interactions by construction (higher reported value cannot reduce acceptance, while a longer reported duration or larger resource requirement cannot increase it); (ii) a generalised critical payment: the lowest accepted value, with all other inputs fixed; and (iii) training by either RL or imitation of an existing rule. Together, monotonicity and the critical payment ensure strategyproofness and individual rationality, while the neural rule provides flexibility and imitation improves training efficiency. Empirically, on a four-resource stream with two partner pairs that a linear difference cannot represent, the neural rule achieves 97.8% of an offline welfare upper bound, compared with 93.8% for a linear learner and 89.0% for first-come, first-served, closing 65% and 80% of the remaining optimality gap, respectively. Moreover, imitation reaches 97.5% in only 261 seconds, about 89 times faster than RL. △ Less

    Authors: Ziyang Chen, Qian Li, Chengwei Qin

    Abstract: GUI memory should preserve the results of past actions and learn reusable operations. We introduce Task Advancement Memory (TAM), which defines task advancement… ▽ More GUI memory should preserve the results of past actions and learn reusable operations. This is difficult in long-horizon workflows, where agents must use information across applications and useful operations are interleaved with failed attempts. We introduce Task Advancement Memory (TAM), which defines task advancement as verified task progress, and supports ongoing task execution and learns reusable operations. During execution, Task Advancement State represents how verified task progress changes the remaining goals, allowing the agent to build on earlier achievements across applications. After each task, advancement supervision identifies and abstracts the interactions that produced a verified local result. The learned operations preserve the relation between required inputs, expected outcomes, and verification conditions. This retains locally successful operations from both successful and failed tasks. On MobileWorld GUI-only tasks, Advancement State alone raises success from 12.0% to 28.2%. Full TAM achieves a 17.6% relative gain in task success over the strongest evaluated baseline and reduces lifecycle tokens per solved task by 38.7%. On SPA-Bench, TAM achieves state-of-the-art task success in the single-app English Level 3 setting. △ Less

    Beyond the Readout: A Latent Numerical Frame Shared Across Modality and Operation

    Authors: Oriol Juan Sabater, Alex Ferrando de las Morenas, Pau Rodríguez, Xavier Suau

    Abstract: …output digits. We therefore use causal interventions. For four conditions (text or image, addition or subtraction), we learn a small subspace of Gemma4-12B-it's residual stream in which swapping coordinates swaps the model's answer. The subspaces answer three questions. First, do the conditions write numbers along the same directions? They do not. Re… ▽ More When a multimodal model computes 17+35 from text or 91-39 from a rendered image, it produces the same answer, 52. Does it use one internal concept of number for both, or separate machinery for each path? Similarity metrics such as RSA or CKA cannot settle this question. They give the same score to identical and to rotated directions, and two representations can look alike only because both prepare the same output digits. We therefore use causal interventions. For four conditions (text or image, addition or subtraction), we learn a small subspace of Gemma4-12B-it's residual stream in which swapping coordinates swaps the model's answer. The subspaces answer three questions. First, do the conditions write numbers along the same directions? They do not. Reusing a subspace from text in an image on the same operation recovers only 53.2% of its causal effect. A single rotation and rescaling maps one subspace onto the other and raises recovery to 91.4%, so the conditions share one geometry expressed in different coordinates. Second, does this shared geometry only reflect the digit the model is about to print? To test this, we split each subspace into 9 directions that the output layer reads to choose the next digit and 13 directions that it cannot see. The 13 hidden directions still carry the number, yet changing them barely alters the digit being written. Third, what are these hidden directions for? A model must emit a multi-digit answer one digit at a time, so it can compute the full result early and keep it in reserve. When two numbers share their first digit, swapping the number in the hidden directions leaves the digit being written intact and redirects the next digit in 77.6% of cases. The model thus forms an abstract numerical concept upstream and unfolds it across time: multimodal representations share meaning through relational geometry across distinct coordinate frames. △ Less

    Memorize When Needed: Decoupled Memory Control for Spatially Consistent Long-Horizon Video Generation

    Authors: Yanjun Guo, Zhengqiang Zhang, Pengfei Wang, Xinyue Liang, Zhiyuan Ma, Lei Zhang

    Abstract: Spatially consistent long-horizon video generation aims to maintain temporal and spatial consistency along predefined camera trajectories. Existing methods mostly entangle memory modeling with video generation, leading to inconsistent content during scene revisits and diminished generative capacity when exploring novel regions, even when trained on extensive annotated data. To address these limitations, we propose a decoupled framework that separates memory conditioning from generation. Our approach significantly reduces training costs while simultaneously enhancing spatial consistency and preserving the generative capacity for novel scene exploration. Specifically, we employ a lightweight, independent memory branch to learn precise spatial consistency from historical observations. We first introduce a hybrid memory representation to capture complementary temporal and spatial cues from generated frames, then leverage a per-frame cross-attention mechanism to ensure each frame is conditioned exclusively on the most spatially relevant historical information, which is injected into the generative model to ensure spatial consistency. When generating new scenes, a camera-aware gating mechanism is proposed to mediate the interaction between memory and generation modules, enabling memory conditioning only when meaningful historical references exist. Compared with the existing method, our method is highly data-efficient, yet the experiments demonstrate that our approach achieves state-of-the-art performance in terms of both visual quality and spatial consistency. During sampling, the failure generator adaptively steers action generation away from failure-prone regions and toward more reliable success modes, with guidance strength determined by the per-diffusion-step distance between success and failure distributions.     Simulations agree: the Milky Way's satellites are disrupting

    Authors: Alexander H. Riley, Nora Shipp, J. Sebastian Monzon, Arpit Arora, Adriana Dropulic, Gray Thoron, Oscar Agertz, Santi Roca-Fàbrega

    Abstract: …rates are comparable to or exceeding those for classical satellites. The ubiquity of mass loss across models presents both an exciting opportunity (a plethora of stellar streams awaiting discovery) and a nuisance (for theorists using satellite counts to constrain dark matter). ▽ More Recent analyses of simulated Milky Way-mass galaxies have highlighted the prevalence of satellites undergoing tidal disruption. However, these have focused on single hydrodynamical codes with a number of limitations, making it unclear how generic this result is. We compare satellite disruption in four models: two cosmological hydrodynamical simulations (Auriga and FIRE), cosmological $N$-body simulations with stellar particle tagging (Aquarius), and a semi-analytic model with varying host disk properties (SatGen). For all systems, we compute the fraction of stellar mass retained by the progenitor ($f_\text{bound}$) at the present day. We find a remarkable agreement between FIRE, Aquarius, and SatGen; all predict that $\sim$20 to 30 per cent of classical-mass satellites have $f_\text{bound} < 0.9$ ($\sim$5 to 10 per cent have $f_\text{bound} < 0.5$). Satellites in Auriga experience substantially more mass loss than other simulations, which we attribute to their massive central galaxies and large satellite sizes prior to infall. In the ultra-faint regime captured by Aquarius and SatGen, disruption rates are comparable to or exceeding those for classical satellites. The ubiquity of mass loss across models presents both an exciting opportunity (a plethora of stellar streams awaiting discovery) and a nuisance (for theorists using satellite counts to constrain dark matter). △ Less

    Authors: Dirk Gütlin, Ryszard Auksztulewicz, Adrien Doerig

Here, we show that this paradox can be overcome. PBNs therefore close a longstanding gap in computational neuroscience by combining two properties that have been largely separated: the brain-inspired learning mechanisms of predictive coding and the representational power of modern deep networks.     Sequential RF Fingerprinting for AIS: Rejecting Unknown Vessels and Insider MMSI Spoofs Days After Enrollment

    Authors: Fawaz Abdul Razak, Bora Turgut, Mason Clark, Yasin Yilmaz

    Abstract: …identity (MMSI), because nothing in the protocol authenticates it; only the transmitter's analog hardware reveals who is really sending. We treat AIS traffic as streams of bursts keyed by claimed MMSI and accumulate physical-layer evidence with a sequential test. Each burst is scored by its k-nearest-neighbor distance to enrolled fingerprints in the feat… ▽ More Any AIS transmitter can claim any vessel's identity (MMSI), because nothing in the protocol authenticates it; only the transmitter's analog hardware reveals who is really sending. We treat AIS traffic as streams of bursts keyed by claimed MMSI and accumulate physical-layer evidence with a sequential test. Each burst is scored by its k-nearest-neighbor distance to enrolled fingerprints in the feature space of a classifier whose weights are fixed after training, calibrated into a tail probability, and accumulated by a geometric-entropy-minimization cumulative-sum (GEM-CUSUM) statistic. The same test rejects unknown transmitters when scored against all enrolled vessels, and exposes insider spoofs when scored against the claimed vessel. A threshold refreshed from genuine traffic alone keeps it at a stated false-alarm rate as fingerprints drift. On AIS in-phase/quadrature (I/Q) recordings captured over 13 days at the Istanbul Strait (Bosphorus), the detector rejects unseen vessels at least 10 days after enrollment. Evaluated over 50-burst trials with every vessel weighted equally, it reaches an area under the ROC curve (AUROC) of 0.956 (95% confidence interval 0.913-0.989), where the best score computed from one burst at a time reaches 0.899. A threshold refreshed from the first quarter of the enrolled vessels' traffic holds 5.3% false alarms (3.0-9.2% across five independently trained encoders) at 88.8% detection. Scored against the claimed MMSI, enrolled transmitters spoofing another vessel are rejected at 0.991, a threat that open-set tests cannot see (AUROC 0.500, chance level). The lightweight Transformer GLFormer, reimplemented from its paper (no code is public) and retrained on the same data, reaches 0.652 in the same detector with its published in-phase input and 0.849 when given the same I/Q input as our encoder. △ Less

    Spine: Compiling Subtask Trees into Structured Context for Long-Horizon Coding Agents

    Authors: Jiahong Xiang, Kunqiu Chen, Lingming Zhang, Yuqun Zhang

    Abstract: Long-horizon coding tasks contain nested subtasks. Typically, agents record interactions sequentially into a growing history, causing context inflation and performance degradation. Compressing or trimming the history without explicit subtask boundaries can discard information needed by unfinished subtasks. Conversely,… ▽ More Long-horizon coding tasks contain nested subtasks. Typically, agents record interactions sequentially into a growing history, causing context inflation and performance degradation. Compressing or trimming the history without explicit subtask boundaries can discard information needed by unfinished subtasks. Conversely, recursive subagent delegation isolates completed subtasks through child agents by assigning every subtask boundary to delegation, which conflates subtask granularity with executing agent choice, adding execution and coordination overhead. We introduce Spine, an interface and runtime that align model context with subtask nesting and completion. An agent declares explicit subtask boundaries through work scopes, allowing the same agent to execute nested subtasks inline while reserving delegation for independent parallel tasks. The runtime organizes execution in SpineTree, a persistent tree recording subtask relations, local interactions, completion states, and returned results. Before each model call, it compiles the tree into model context just in time, retaining unfinished subtasks' interactions and replacing completed subtasks' details with their results, while preserving prefix-cache reuse. We integrate Spine into Codex to build SpineCodex. With GPT-5.6 Sol, compared with native Codex, SpineCodex improves SWE-Milestone Score by 11.8% while reducing total cost by 32.4%, improves the average ProgramBench test-pass rate by 17.3%, and improves Mean@5 on eight of the ten evaluated FrontierSWE tasks. On SWE-Milestone, it also achieves a comparable Score to Recursive Subagent at 73.7% lower total cost. We also integrate Spine into Pi through a reusable software package: with Gemini 3.8 Flash and Qwen 3.8 27B, it improves SWE-Milestone Score by 9.5-17.5% and the average ProgramBench test-pass rate by 4.3-8.7% compared with native Pi. △ Less

    cs.AI

    Does Your Generalist Skill Optimizer Really Incentivize Agentic Tasks? Rethinking Skill Optimization via Bipartite Attribution

    Authors: Jiawei Zhou, Bo Peng, Yuxuan Huang, Ming Zhang, Youbang Sun, Xiang Wang, Chaochao Lu

    Abstract: Skill optimizers adapt an agent to long-horizon tasks, such as repairing a terminal environment, by rewriting its skill, a natural-language procedure in its context, and keeping the edits that raise success on a small dev set. On such tasks success is noisy, so a gain on a small dev set does not show that an edit helps… ▽ More Skill optimizers adapt an agent to long-horizon tasks, such as repairing a terminal environment, by rewriting its skill, a natural-language procedure in its context, and keeping the edits that raise success on a small dev set. On such tasks success is noisy, so a gain on a small dev set does not show that an edit helps, and a success score does not show what the edit costs. We introduce BiPA, which replays an optimizer's acceptance rule on candidates identical to its seed to measure what selection alone reports, and records each episode as a bipartite graph of turns and the text they read to trace extra input tokens to the behavior behind them. Across three optimizers, three agentic benchmarks and two models, the learned skills succeed about as often as their hand-written seed, 0.1 points higher on average. Their dev gains vanish on test: keeping the best of several candidates on at most 30 tasks reports gains this large even when none changed. They read about 16% more input tokens: the optimizers almost always add rules, half to re-check work and some to act one call at a time, and the agent follows them as extra turns, each re-sending the whole conversation. Four principles follow: accept an edit only if its gain exceeds what selection alone reports; charge it for the turns it adds, not for its length; let the editor delete and merge rules; show it what earlier rules cost. Code is available at https://github.com/valleysprings/BiPA.git. △ Less

    PipeLink: Contract-Guided Linking of GPU Kernel Components

    Authors: Jize Guo, Xupeng Miao

    Abstract: …machine-learning kernels are compositions of components, such as loaders, matrix-multiply mainloops, epilogues and communication stages, that run concurrently on one GPU streaming multiprocessor and share its on-chip memory, barriers, warps and registers. Because these components hard-code where they sit in this shared space, they cannot be compiled, shipped… ▽ More The fastest machine-learning kernels are compositions of components, such as loaders, matrix-multiply mainloops, epilogues and communication stages, that run concurrently on one GPU streaming multiprocessor and share its on-chip memory, barriers, warps and registers. Because these components hard-code where they sit in this shared space, they cannot be compiled, shipped or replaced on their own: combining components from different sources means forking them by hand or keeping them in separate kernels. We present PipeLink, a contract-guided linker for GPU kernel components. Each component is compiled separately into an LTO object that carries a contract stating what it requires and what it promises; PipeLink checks the contracts against each other, relocates every component by solving one joint placement of the SM's resources, and generates the transfers between them, and the vendor LTO backend folds the result into constants. Across 51 configurations on Hopper and Blackwell GPUs, linked kernels run within 0.2% of the same sources compiled as one unit. Linking makes new compositions practical: a CuTe attention operator inside a ThunderKittens decode megakernel speeds up decoding by 2.16$\times$, a four-owner MoE training megakernel matches the original monolith, one unchanged attention core yields sparse and context-parallel variants that outperform FlexAttention, vLLM and Transformer Engine, and a GEMM + SwiGLU composition whose consumer serves three GPU architectures runs 1.2$\times$ faster on Blackwell than cuBLAS with a compiled activation. △ Less

    Action-Value Calibration via Continuation Sharing for Long-Horizon Agentic Reinforcement Learning

    Authors: Yingjian Chen, Lang Zhou, Zixin Zhao, Pingfan Li, Weishi Zheng, Zhilin Zhao

    Abstract: Reinforcement learning for long-horizon agents relies on feedback from sequences of interactions, making credit assignment to individual actions difficult. Existing methods such as GiGPO compare actions at shared states to compute relative advantages, but estimate each action's value from a single sampled continuat… ▽ More Reinforcement learning for long-horizon agents relies on feedback from sequences of interactions, making credit assignment to individual actions difficult. Existing methods such as GiGPO compare actions at shared states to compute relative advantages, but estimate each action's value from a single sampled continuation. The resulting local credit remains sensitive to decisions made later in the trajectory. To address this problem, we propose ConCal (Continuation Calibration), a recursive continuation-value estimator that incorporates continuation evidence already present in a rollout batch. ConCal matches visits from the same task by their incoming state-action-state transitions, combines shared continuation evidence with each visit's own transition estimate, and propagates calibrated values backward to refine earlier action-value estimates. This reduces dependence on individual sampled continuations and mitigates local credit distortions without additional rollouts. Held-out return diagnostics show lower error in within-group value differences and higher agreement with reference action orderings than estimates based on individual continuations. With Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct, ConCal improves success rates over GiGPO by 3.39 and 5.86 percentage points on ALFWorld and WebShop, respectively. Our code is available at https://github.com/Yingjian-Chen/ConCal. △ Less

Existing function-on-function regression methods are primarily designed for batch data, and bootstrap inference has become one of the most popular approaches. However, this procedure may be infeasible when observations cannot be stored. In this paper, we propose a one-pass functional SGD estimator that updates upon each observation's arrival without data storage, and a weighted rolling validation rule is provided for step size selection for implementation. Asymptotic behaviors are established for the proposed method, and conditional bootstrap validity is justified for the bias-centered pointwise estimation error. Simulation studies and two real data applications confirm the empirical effectiveness of the proposed procedures. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first theoretically justified one-pass framework for bootstrap inference on a bivariate coefficient surface with streaming functional data. △ Less

Evaluated across diverse real-world workloads, we significantly outperform the current state of the art in both compression ratio and que… ▽ More Graph-based Approximate Nearest Neighbor (ANN) indexes, such as HNSW, are the backbone of modern vector search. However, their massive memory footprint creates a severe infrastructure bottleneck. While vector quantization is widely adopted to reduce the memory required to store data vectors, the graph's edge structure increasingly dominates total memory usage. Compressing these adjacency lists for online search presents a critical system challenge: because decompression occurs during query processing, i.e., while navigating the graph, memory savings cannot come at the expense of query latency. Evaluated across diverse real-world workloads, we significantly outperform the current state of the art in both compression ratio and query latency. Our experimental results show that we achieve compression ratios up to 52.7% with negligible penalties in latency compared to the uncompressed baseline. Furthermore, our technique has been effectively deployed in production at eBay, achieving up to 44.3% reduction in edge storage with no degradation in query latency. △ Less

    cs.LG cs.AI cs.CL

    The Geometry of Learning: Read-Write Asymmetry Across Training

    Authors: Valeria Ruscio, Keiran Thompson

    Abstract: Pretraining and alignment update the same transformer weights, and we ask in which directions each stage changes them. The residual stream gives every matrix a shared coordinate system, in which a weight change can be compared with the principal subspace of the activations the matrix reads and with the prediction subspace of the unembedding. In this frame, a… ▽ More Pretraining and alignment update the same transformer weights, and we ask in which directions each stage changes them. The residual stream gives every matrix a shared coordinate system, in which a weight change can be compared with the principal subspace of the activations the matrix reads and with the prediction subspace of the unembedding. In this frame, alignment updates to the query and key matrices are enriched in the principal input subspace, while its updates to the output matrices are close to random relative to the prediction subspace, and the same signature appears in late pretraining and under every objective we apply to one checkpoint, so it is a property of the training stage rather than of the objective. The gradient of a linear map is a sum of outer products $δ_t a_t^\top$, and the residual stream enters this product through the activation $a_t$ for matrices that read from it and through the upstream gradient $δ_t$ for matrices that write into it. Read gradients therefore inherit the spectrum of the activations, which is the same under every objective, while write gradients inherit the geometry of the upstream gradients, which point into the prediction subspace only early in pretraining, when the output matrices acquire their prediction geometry. The raw read gradient follows the activation spectrum, the optimiser attenuates the concentration it inherits, and cancellation between tokens sets the scale of the update but not its direction. A write direction derived from the unembedding still changes predictions after alignment, and adaptation restricted to any single group of matrices remains effective, so the signature describes where training places its updates, not which matrices can carry them. △ Less

    AgentSentry: Mitigating Indirect Prompt Injection in LLM Agents via Temporal Causal Diagnostics and Context Purification

    Authors: Tian Zhang, Yiwei Xu, Juan Wang, Keyan Guo, Xiaoyang Xu, Bowen Xiao, Quanlong Guan, Jinlin Fan, Jiawei Liu, Zhiquan Liu, Hongxin Hu

    Abstract: …Under Attack (UA) of 76.10%. Over the strongest evaluated baseline, it improves average UA by 1.84--7.60 percentage points, with benign FPRs of 2.06%--5.15%. We further evaluate long-horizon and adaptive attacks, imperfect purification, runtime overhead, and an OpenClaw case study. ▽ More Tool-augmented large language model (LLM) agents aggregate dialogue history, tool outputs, and retrieved content into an accumulated runtime state to complete multi-step tasks. This execution model exposes agents to indirect prompt injection (IPI) attacks, in which attacker-controlled instructions embedded in external artifacts enter the agent state and later influence tool use. Multi-turn IPI is especially challenging because injected content can persist across steps and trigger unauthorized state-changing actions only after merging with user intent. This creates a source-attribution problem: existing inference-time defenses can often detect suspicious behavior but do not directly distinguish whether a pending action is supported by the user task or influenced by injected content. They therefore resort to conservative blocking, termination, or broad filtering. We propose AgentSentry, an inference-time defense that operationalizes temporal causal diagnostics through boundary-local counterfactual re-executions at tool-return boundaries. It substitutes the mediator under a restored snapshot, records whether the pending proposal survives, and aggregates user-view and probe scores across adjacent boundaries. When a proposal is mediator-sensitive, AgentSentry purifies the implicated mediator content while preserving task-relevant evidence and resumes execution from the sanitized state. We evaluate AgentSentry on AgentDojo across four task suites, three IPI attack families, and three black-box LLM backbones. It records no observed successful attack in the evaluated cases (ASR = 0%) and achieves an average Utility Under Attack (UA) of 76.10%. Over the strongest evaluated baseline, it improves average UA by 1.84--7.60 percentage points, with benign FPRs of 2.06%--5.15%. We further evaluate long-horizon and adaptive attacks, imperfect purification, runtime overhead, and an OpenClaw case study. △ Less

On the test set, at the default 2-s prediction interval with refinement enabled, inhalation detection achieved a sensitivity of 0.828, a positive predictive value of 0.832, and an F1-score of 0.830. Airmod also broadcasts the noise-cancelled tracheal sound and displays a real-time spectrogram to help clinicians recognize respiratory abnormalities. △ Less

    The dynamical memory of tidal stellar streams: Joint inference of the Galactic potential and the progenitor of GD-1 with flow matching

    Authors: Giuseppe Viterbo, Tobias Buck

    Abstract: Stellar streams offer one of the most sensitive probes of the Milky Way`s gravitational potential, as their phase-space morphology encodes both the tidal field of the host galaxy and the internal structure of their progenitors. In this work, we introduce a framework that leverages Flow Matching and Simulation-Based Inference (SBI) to jointly infer the parame… ▽ More Stellar streams offer one of the most sensitive probes of the Milky Way`s gravitational potential, as their phase-space morphology encodes both the tidal field of the host galaxy and the internal structure of their progenitors. In this work, we introduce a framework that leverages Flow Matching and Simulation-Based Inference (SBI) to jointly infer the parameters of the GD-1 progenitor and the global properties of the Milky Way potential. Our aim is to move beyond traditional techniques (e.g. orbit-fitting and action-angle methods) by constructing a fully Bayesian, likelihood-free posterior over both host-galaxy parameters and progenitor properties, thereby capturing the intrinsic coupling between tidal stripping dynamics and the underlying potential. To achieve this, we generate a large suite of mock GD-1-like streams using our differentiable N-body code \textsc{\texttt{Odisseo}}, sampling self-consistent initial conditions from a Plummer sphere and evolving them in a flexible Milky Way potential model. We then apply conditional Flow Matching to learn the vector field that transports a base Gaussian distribution into the posterior, enabling efficient, amortized inference directly from stream phase-space data. We demonstrate that our method successfully recovers the true parameters of a fiducial GD-1 simulation, producing well-calibrated posteriors and accurately reproducing parameter degeneracies arising from progenitor-host interactions. Flow Matching provides a powerful, flexible framework for Galactic Archaeology. Our approach enables joint inference on progenitor and Galactic parameters, capturing complex dependencies that are difficult to model with classical likelihood-based methods. △ Less

Enhancement therefore requires a single forward pass per frame and no iterative sampling or look-ahead. We further truncate the codec's residual vector quantizer (RVQ) to its coarse levels, halving the number of tokens per frame while improving robustness to reverberation. StreamSE is the only streaming generative model in our comparison, yet it matches or exceeds the perceptual quality of the offline generative baselines. Furthermore, the complete StreamSE pipeline runs in real time on a single smartphone CPU core. Our results show that continuous latent regression provides a practical route to generative SE under fully causal, on-device streaming constraints. △ Less

    MemPrism: Task-Conditioned Relational Memory Views for Long-Horizon Agents

    Authors: Bingfan Zeng, Zhisheng Chen, Zhengwei Xie, Bangde Cao, Yuxuan Li, Jinhan Li, Zheng Lu, Xiangchen Guan, Zikai Xiao, Rui Qian, Jingwei Song

    Abstract: Long-… ▽ More Long-horizon agents performing multi-step Web tasks must reuse earlier page observations and interactions to support subsequent actions. However, preserving relevant history does not ensure that it exposes the relations needed for the current decision. We call this gap representation mismatch. To this end, we propose MemPrism, a task-conditioned relational memory framework that separates persistent experience storage from decision-time working memory. MemPrism records interactions as the event stream and dynamically constructs relational views according to the current task context. A lightweight view policy selects the relation structure, evidence range, outcome condition, and granularity, while a deterministic composer and renderer transform historical facts into a temporary optical working-memory view for a frozen task policy. Experiments on long-horizon embodied and web-agent benchmarks show that MemPrism consistently improves the task performance, especially as trajectories become longer, while reducing memory token consumption. Furthermore, the learned view policy transfers across different VLMs without additional adaptation, demonstrating the effectiveness of task-conditioned relational views as a general memory interface for agents. △ Less

    AutoVend: Benchmarking LLM Agents on Long-Horizon Autonomous Vending Operations

    Authors: Qiming Shi, Yikun Li, Beidi Luan, Rui Sun, Yan Ma, Jing Li, Daxin Jiang, Zuo Bai

    Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents are extending agent-mediated commerce on the Web from shopping for consumers to operating businesses for sellers. In online-to-offline (O2O) commerce, decisions are issued through Web-based platforms but realized through costly physical execution. Evaluating such agents requires going beyond individual transactions to ask whether decisions made online turn into su… ▽ More Large language model (LLM) agents are extending agent-mediated commerce on the Web from shopping for consumers to operating businesses for sellers. In online-to-offline (O2O) commerce, decisions are issued through Web-based platforms but realized through costly physical execution. Evaluating such agents requires going beyond individual transactions to ask whether decisions made online turn into sustained economic returns. We introduce AutoVend, a benchmark in which one LLM agent manages 20 vending machines across five site types through a Web-based operating interface for 180 simulated days. Its data-grounded simulator combines a shared warehouse, procurement lead times, paid replenishment, inventory expiration, and recurring operating costs. We evaluate five LLM agents against a rule-based inventory-control baseline and diagnose their site coordination, execution, and performance over time. Four of five evaluated LLM agents fall short of this baseline, and returns per dollar of inference cost rank models differently from returns alone. Operational analyses show that gains in individual metrics do not consistently yield stronger economic growth, as inventory and execution costs erode these gains. Trajectory analysis further reveals difficulties in grounding feasible actions, managing inventory commitments, and converting feedback into effective policy revisions. △ Less

    physics.flu-dyn physics.comp-ph

    Local Metrics for Exact Streaming on Curvilinear Lattices: A Curvilinear D3Q27 Cumulant Lattice-Boltzmann Method

    Authors: Maher Sawaf, Jonas Latt, Nikolaus A. Adams, Mostafa S. Shadloo

    Abstract: A three-dimensional curvilinear lattice-Boltzmann method is introduced for simulations on smoothly deformed grids while retaining exact one-to-one streaming on a uniform computational lattice. The coordinate transformation is represented locally through a metric-Gaussian equilibrium and a Christoffel-connection source in moment space. A dynamically reconstru… ▽ More A three-dimensional curvilinear lattice-Boltzmann method is introduced for simulations on smoothly deformed grids while retaining exact one-to-one streaming on a uniform computational lattice. The coordinate transformation is represented locally through a metric-Gaussian equilibrium and a Christoffel-connection source in moment space. A dynamically reconstructed homogeneous-state perturbation streaming procedure preserves homogeneous physical states on warped meshes without interpolation in the advection step. The method is implemented on a D3Q27 lattice with a cumulant collision operator, while the geometric construction remains compatible with other local collision models. The formulation is assessed using two sets of tests. First, static non-orthogonal warped-cube cases validate the bulk curvilinear solver through a convected helical shear wave, the Taylor--Green vortex, and Arnold--Beltrami--Childress flow. These tests verify viscous decay, clean freestream preservation, vortex-breakdown dynamics, and the nonlinear inertial--pressure balance induced by geometric acceleration. Second, wall-bounded and moving-boundary demonstrations assess the boundary-compatible extension through warped plane Poiseuille flow, arbitrary Lagrangian--Eulerian (ALE) peristaltic pumping, and a two-way fluid--structure interaction (FSI) case with added-mass frequency shift. The results show that the proposed curvilinear LBM avoids interpolation-based transport, reduces geometry-locked numerical modes, preserves global mass and momentum to round-off accuracy in the tested warped periodic cases, and extends to wall-bounded ALE/FSI configurations with conservative treatment of mass and physically consistent momentum exchange. △ Less

    Abstract: …before being matched to current conditions. On ProactiveStream, ProactMem achieves an overall effectiveness (F1) of 52.6%, a 21.4% relative improvement over the stro… ▽ More Proactive LLM agents anticipate service opportunities from ongoing observations and accumulated history. Existing approaches emphasize recent context or retrieval of related memories, providing limited support for validating recurring behavioral regularities and assessing their applicability to the current event. We propose Proactive Memory (ProactMem), which organizes history into recent events, temporal trajectories, and behavioral regularities. Trajectory facts are checked against source events, and regularities are assessed through historical replay before being matched to current conditions. We also construct ProactiveStream, a longitudinal benchmark covering three application scopes, where agents process isolated event streams chronologically under episode-aware opportunity labels. On ProactiveStream, ProactMem achieves an overall effectiveness (F1) of 52.6%, a 21.4% relative improvement over the strongest of five external baselines. Code is available at: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/ProactMem-5351/. △ Less

    cs.LG

    Live-OPSD: On-Policy Self-Distillation for Proactive Streaming Large Video Models

    Authors: Kaixuan Du, Xin Wan, Dai Guan, Ming Chen, Xiaoxi Jiang, Guanjun Jiang

    Abstract: Streaming multimodal assistants must make response decisions while events are still unfolding. They need to recognize when the available evidence warrants a response, produce an appropriate continuation, and remain silent otherwise. Supervised fine-tuning learns from recorded assistant turns, but does not directly train on the response prefixes produced by t… ▽ More Streaming multimodal assistants must make response decisions while events are still unfolding. They need to recognize when the available evidence warrants a response, produce an appropriate continuation, and remain silent otherwise. Supervised fine-tuning learns from recorded assistant turns, but does not directly train on the response prefixes produced by the evolving policy itself. We introduce Live-OPSD, an on-policy self-distillation method that uses privileged hints available only during training to supervise the policy on its own outputs. The policy first generates a continuation from the streaming context alone. The same model then re-evaluates that continuation with a hint describing the relevant evidence, and the resulting distributions guide the unhinted policy. On Qwen3-Omni-30B-A3B, Live-OPSD attains an average score of 59.64 on LiveProBench, 5.26 points above the strongest continued-SFT baseline, and stays competitive on OVO-Bench (65.84). These results indicate that guiding the policy on its own outputs with privileged hints improves both when and how a streaming assistant responds. △ Less

    Mining the Nineteenth Century for the Thirteenth: Register-Scale Forced Alignment and HTR Bootstrapping via Legacy Regesta and Antiquarian Editions

    Authors: Tamas Kovacs, Daniel Luger

    Abstract: …Pressutti's regests, the closing datum clauses, and the regests' dates converted to the chancery's Roman form find 73.9% of entry starts in the recognized line stream, and edition agreement estimates a CER of 7.80% beyond fol. 203. Antiquarian scholarship thus cannot replace modern transcription, but it can steer, check, and segment HTR across t… ▽ More Handwritten text recognition (HTR) for the registers of Pope Honorius III depends on modern expert transcriptions, which exist only for regnal years 1-2 of Reg. Vat. 9. For the remaining registers, nineteenth-century scholarship offers Pressutti's regesta and partial editions. On fol. 1-203, where both layers exist, we measure what this legacy layer can contribute, on transcriptions that we first audit against it, and find a division of labor across three tiers. At the orthographic tier, raw editions are at best neutral for adapting a recognizer to the second regnal year and harmful without replay, whereas projection onto curial spelling with replay of modern source lines turns them into zero-label adaptation targets: pooled over all 134 year-2 sides, the WER falls by 1.50 points (95% CI -1.84 to -1.15), closing 33% of the gap to an oracle; the spelling changes at the year split, through the scribe or the transcriber, whereas the hand changes visibly only at fol. 191r. At the lexical tier, the corpus-wide legacy lexicon contains 82.2% of the out-of-vocabulary words of unedited entries and flags recognition errors with 66.7% precision, and labels from pre-trained medieval NER models, distilled into the recognizer's NER head, reach F1 82.1% on an expert validated benchmark, level with the best tagger applied after recognition. At the topological tier, Pressutti's regests, the closing datum clauses, and the regests' dates converted to the chancery's Roman form find 73.9% of entry starts in the recognized line stream, and edition agreement estimates a CER of 7.80% beyond fol. 203. Antiquarian scholarship thus cannot replace modern transcription, but it can steer, check, and segment HTR across the untranscribed registers. △ Less

    OdysseyArena: Benchmarking Large Language Models For Long-Horizon, Active and Inductive Interactions

    Authors: Fangzhi Xu, Hang Yan, Qiushi Sun, Jinyang Wu, Zixian Huang, Muye Huang, Jingyang Gong, Zichen Ding, Kanzhi Cheng, Yian Wang, Xinyu Che, Zeyi Sun, Jian Zhang, Zhangyue Yin, Haoran Luo, Ben Kao, Qika Lin

    Abstract: …which is the cornerstone for enabling agentic foresight and sustaining strategic coherence. To bridge this gap, we introduce OdysseyArena, which re-centers agent evaluation on long-… ▽ More The advancement of Large Language Models (LLMs) has catalyzed the development of autonomous agents capable of navigating complex environments. However, existing evaluations primarily adopt a deductive paradigm, where agents execute tasks based on explicitly provided rules and static goals, often within limited planning horizons. Crucially, this neglects the inductive necessity for agents to discover latent transition laws from experience autonomously, which is the cornerstone for enabling agentic foresight and sustaining strategic coherence. To bridge this gap, we introduce OdysseyArena, which re-centers agent evaluation on long-horizon, active, and inductive interactions. We formalize and instantiate four primitives, translating abstract transition dynamics into concrete interactive environments. Building upon this, we establish OdysseyArena-Lite for standardized benchmarking, providing a set of 120 tasks to measure an agent's inductive efficiency and long-horizon discovery. Pushing further, we introduce OdysseyArena-Challenge to stress-test agent stability across extreme interaction horizons (e.g., > 200 steps). Extensive experiments on 15+ leading LLMs reveal that even frontier models exhibit a deficiency in inductive scenarios, identifying a critical bottleneck in the pursuit of autonomous discovery in complex environments. Project homepage: https://os-copilot.github.io/OdysseyArena-Home/ △ Less

    cs.LG

    Trajectory-Aware Policy Optimization: Closing the State Distribution Gap in Long-Horizon LLM RL

    Authors: Zhicheng Cai, Hanlin Wu, Weinan Dai, Yuyan Zhou, Wei-Ying Ma, Ya-Qin Zhang, Hao Zhou

    Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has become a dominant paradigm for enhancing LLMs' reasoning capabilities. Yet long-horizon autoregressive rollouts introduce a fundamental limitation: local policy mismatch can compound into severe state distribution shift over trajectories. Existing LLM RL methods, such as GRPO and GSP… ▽ More Reinforcement learning (RL) has become a dominant paradigm for enhancing LLMs' reasoning capabilities. Yet long-horizon autoregressive rollouts introduce a fundamental limitation: local policy mismatch can compound into severe state distribution shift over trajectories. Existing LLM RL methods, such as GRPO and GSPO, control policy mismatch at the token or sequence level, but overlook this trajectory-induced shift, causing training instability and eventual collapse as the horizon grows. To close the state distribution gap, we propose Trajectory-Aware Policy Optimization (TAPO), a principled framework that theoretically derives the state-distribution correction from the off-policy policy gradient and incorporates it into the RL objective. TAPO further employs a geometric-mean estimator for variance reduction, together with a length-aware dynamic clipping that prevents overly deviated updates across the trajectory. Extensive experiments demonstrate that TAPO significantly surpasses existing LLM RL algorithms across eight competition-level benchmarks, achieving up to 48% and 12% improvement over GRPO on mathematical reasoning and algorithmic coding, with substantially enhanced training stability. △ Less
